    License: arXiv.org perpetual non-exclusive license
  
  

arXiv:2404.18364v2 [math.PR] 22 May 2025




Interface motion from Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics 
of non-gradient type



Tadahisa Funaki


August 24, 2026


Abstract
    

We consider the Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics on a
dd-dimensional periodic lattice of size NN, that is,
a stochastic time evolution of particles performing random walks with interaction
subject to the exclusion rule (Kawasaki part), in general,
of non-gradient type, together with the effect of the
creation and annihilation of particles
(Glauber part) whose rates are set to favor two levels of particle density, called
sparse and dense. We then study the limit of our dynamics under
the hydrodynamic space-time scaling, that is,
1/N1/N in space and a diffusive scaling N2N^{2} for the Kawasaki part and
another scaling K=K⁡(N)K=K(N), which diverges slower, for the Glauber part in time.
In the limit as N→∞N\to\infty, we show that
the particles autonomously make phase separation into sparse or dense
phases at the microscopic level, and an interface separating two regions
is formed at the macroscopic level and evolves under an anisotropic
curvature flow.
    

In the present article, we show that the particle density at the macroscopic level
is well approximated by a solution of a reaction-diffusion equation with a nonlinear
diffusion term of divergence form and a large reaction term.
Furthermore, by applying the results of Funaki, Gu and Wang [arXiv:2404.12234]
for the convergence rate of the diffusion matrix approximated by local functions,
we obtain a quantitative hydrodynamic limit as well as the upper bound for the
allowed diverging speed of K=K⁡(N)K=K(N).
    

The above result for the derivation of the interface motion
is proved by combining our result with that in a companion paper
by Funaki and Park [arXiv:2403.01732], in which we analyzed the asymptotic
behavior of the solution of the reaction-diffusion equation obtained in the
present article and derived
an anisotropic curvature flow in the situation where the macroscopic
reaction term determined from the Glauber part is bistable and balanced.
    ††
        
      Beijing Institute of Mathematical Sciences and Applications,
No. 544 Hefangkou, Huairou District, Beijing 101408, China.
e-mail: funaki@ms.u-tokyo.ac.jp 
    ††
        
      Abbreviated title: Interface motion from Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics
    ††
        
      MSC2020: 60K35, 82C22, 74A50.
    ††
        
      Keywords: Kawasaki dynamics, exclusion process, Glauber dynamics,
hydrodynamic limit, non-gradient model, anisotropic curvature flow,
interface problem, phase separation.
  




1 Introduction — model and results




The present article studies the hydrodynamic behavior of the Glauber-Kawasaki
dynamics with a diverging scaling parameter KK in the Glauber part.
The Kawasaki part governs the time evolution of particles moving as
interacting random
walks subject to the exclusion rule. It is generally of non-gradient type
and we assume its reversibility under Bernoulli measures.
The Glauber part prescribes the law of creation and annihilation of particles.
Here we quote the original papers [21] by Glauber and [24] by
Kawasaki initially
designed for stochastic dynamics corresponding to the Ising model.
We show that, in particular in the situation
that the particles have two favorable stable phases called ‘sparse’ and ‘dense’ with
different mean densities, they autonomously undergo phase separation into one of
these two phases at the
microscopic level, and an interface separating the two regions is
formed at the macroscopic level and it evolves under an anisotropic curvature flow;
see Theorem 1.4.




To show this, in the present article, we establish that the particle density
at the macroscopic level is well approximated by the solution of a reaction-diffusion
equation with nonlinear diffusion and a diverging reaction term;
see Theorem 1.1. The asymptotic behavior of the solution
of this equation is studied in a separate article [17] based on a method
in partial differential equations (PDEs);
see Theorems 1.2 and 1.3.
Combining these results, we can complete the derivation of the
interface motion from our particle systems. Note that the PDE is used only
secondarily.




The present article extends the results obtained in [15],
[8] for the Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics
or the Glauber-Zero range process of gradient type to a model
of non-gradient type. Moreover, applying the results in [14],
we obtain a quantitative hydrodynamic limit with its convergence rate in
Theorem 1.1
and also the upper bound for the allowed diverging speed of K=K⁡(N)K=K(N) in
the Glauber part.




1.1 Model at microscopic level




Let us formulate our model.
We consider the Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics on a dd-dimensional
periodic square lattice 𝕋Nd=(ℤ/N​ℤ)d≡{1,2,…,N}d{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}=({\mathbb{Z}}/N{\mathbb{Z}})^{d}\equiv\{1,2,\ldots,N\}^{d} of large
size N∈ℕ≡{1,2,…}N\in{\mathbb{N}}\equiv\{1,2,\ldots\}. The generator ℒN\mathcal{L}_{N}
of our dynamics is given by the sum of those of Kawasaki and Glauber dynamics with
time change factors N2N^{2} and K=K⁡(N)≥1K=K(N)\geq 1, respectively:




(1.1)

ℒN=N2​LE+K​LG.\mathcal{L}_{N}=N^{2}L_{E}+KL_{G}.



The Kawasaki part (also called the exclusion process part)
is the same as in [19], [11]. It is of non-gradient
type and reversible under Bernoulli measures; see (1.2) and
the conditions (1)–(3) below. The hydrodynamic scaling limit for
the Kawasaki dynamics reversible under Gibbs measures was studied in [35].
In [15], [16], the so-called gradient
condition was assumed for the Kawasaki part
but here we discuss without assuming it. The large deviation principle
corresponding to [35] was shown in [2].




To define the operators LEL_{E} and LGL_{G} precisely, we introduce
several notations. The configuration space of the dynamics
is 𝒳N={0,1}𝕋Nd\mathcal{X}_{N}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}} whose element is
denoted by η={ηx;x∈𝕋Nd}\eta=\{\eta_{x};x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}\} where
ηx=0\eta_{x}=0 or 11 indicates that the site xx is vacant or
occupied, respectively.
We denote ℱN\mathcal{F}_{N} the set of all functions
on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N}. Let τx\tau_{x}, x∈𝕋Ndx\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}, be the shift
operators acting on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} by (τx​η)y=ηy+x,y∈𝕋Nd,(\tau_{x}\eta)_{y}=\eta_{y+x},y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}, where addition is modulo NN. They also act on ℱN\mathcal{F}_{N} by
(τx​f)​(η)=f⁡(τx​η),f∈ℱN(\tau_{x}f)(\eta)=f(\tau_{x}\eta),f\in\mathcal{F}_{N}.




For x,y∈𝕋Ndx,y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}
and η∈𝒳N\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}, ηx,y\eta^{x,y} denotes the element of 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N},
obtained from η\eta by exchanging the values of ηx\eta_{x} and ηy\eta_{y},
that is,





(ηx,y)z={ηy, if ​z=x,ηx, if ​z=y,ηz, if ​z≠x,y.\displaystyle(\eta^{x,y})_{z}=\left\{\begin{aligned} \eta_{y},&\quad\text{ if }z=x,\\
\eta_{x},&\quad\text{ if }z=y,\\
\eta_{z},&\quad\text{ if }z\not=x,y.\end{aligned}\right.



For x∈𝕋Ndx\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}
and η∈𝒳N\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}, ηx\eta^{x} denotes the element of 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N},
obtained from η\eta by flipping the value of ηx\eta_{x}, that is,





(ηx)z={1−ηx, if ​z=x,ηz, if ​z≠x.\displaystyle(\eta^{x})_{z}=\left\{\begin{aligned} 1-\eta_{x},&\quad\text{ if }z=x,\\
\eta_{z},\;\;\;&\quad\text{ if }z\not=x.\end{aligned}\right.






The notations τx\tau_{x}, ηx,y\eta^{x,y} and ηx\eta^{x}, x,y∈ℤdx,y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} also indicate
the corresponding ones for 𝒳={0,1}ℤd\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}, the configuration space
on the whole lattice ℤd{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}. For Λ⊂𝕋Nd\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d} or ⊂ℤd\subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{d},
(Λ)∗(\Lambda)^{*} denotes the set of all (undirected) bonds b={x,y}b=\{x,y\} inside Λ\Lambda,
i.e., x,y∈Λx,y\in\Lambda and |x−y|=1|x-y|=1. Throughout the paper, we use
the norm |z||z| for z=(zi)i=1d∈ℤdz=(z_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} in ℓ∞\ell^{\infty}-sense:
|z|=max1≤i≤d⁡|zi||z|=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}|z_{i}|.
We sometimes write ηb\eta^{b}
instead of ηx,y\eta^{x,y} for bonds b={x,y}b=\{x,y\}.




The generator LEL_{E} of the Kawasaki part (exclusion process part)
on 𝕋Nd{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d} is defined as




(1.2)

LE=∑b∈(𝕋Nd)∗cb(η)πb=12∑x,y∈𝕋Nd:|x−y|=1cx,y(η)πx,y,L_{E}=\sum_{b\in({\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d})^{*}}c_{b}(\eta)\pi_{b}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}:|x-y|=1}c_{x,y}(\eta)\pi_{x,y},



where πb≡πx,y,b={x,y},\pi_{b}\equiv\pi_{x,y},b=\{x,y\}, is the exchange operator
on ℱN\mathcal{F}_{N} defined by





πb​f​(η)=f⁡(ηb)−f⁡(η),f∈ℱN.\pi_{b}f(\eta)=f(\eta^{b})-f(\eta),\quad f\in\mathcal{F}_{N}.



The functions {cb(η)≡cx,y(η);b={x,y}∈(ℤd)∗}\{c_{b}(\eta)\equiv c_{x,y}(\eta);b=\{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{d})^{*}\} are defined on 𝒳\mathcal{X} and determine the
jump (or exchange) rates of particles between two neighboring sites
xx and yy. We assume that they satisfy the following three conditions (1)–(3):


(1) 



Non-degeneracy and locality: cx,y​(η)>0c_{x,y}(\eta)>0 and it is local,
that is, it depends only on {ηz;|z−x|≤rc}\{\eta_{z};|z-x|\leq r_{c}\} for some rc>0r_{c}>0.


(2) 



Spatial homogeneity: cx,y=τx​c0,y−xc_{x,y}=\tau_{x}c_{0,y-x} for every
{x,y}∈(ℤd)∗\{x,y\}\in({\mathbb{Z}}^{d})^{*}.


(3) 



Detailed balance under Bernoulli measures: cx,y​(η)c_{x,y}(\eta)
does not depend on {ηx,ηy}\{\eta_{x},\eta_{y}\}.



In view of (1), the jump rate cb​(η)c_{b}(\eta) is naturally regarded as a
function on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} for b∈(𝕋Nd)∗b\in({\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d})^{*}, at least if NN is large
enough such that N>2​rcN>2r_{c}.
The third condition (3) is equivalent to the symmetricity of LEL_{E}
with respect to the Bernoulli measures νρN,ρ∈[0,1]\nu_{\rho}^{N},\rho\in[0,1],
that is, the product probability measures on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} such that
νρN​(ηx=1)=ρ\nu_{\rho}^{N}(\eta_{x}=1)=\rho for every x∈𝕋Ndx\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}. We will denote
the Bernoulli measures on 𝒳\mathcal{X} by νρ\nu_{\rho}, under which the
operator LEL_{E} considered on ℤd{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} is symmetric; see [13], [15].
Note that we do not assume the gradient condition; cf. [13], [15].




On the other hand, the generator LGL_{G} of the Glauber part is
given by





LG=∑x∈𝕋Ndcx​(η)​πx,L_{G}=\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}c_{x}(\eta)\pi_{x},



where πx\pi_{x}, x∈𝕋Ndx\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}, is the flip operator on ℱN\mathcal{F}_{N}
defined by





πx​f​(η)=f⁡(ηx)−f⁡(η),f∈ℱN.\pi_{x}f(\eta)=f(\eta^{x})-f(\eta),\quad f\in\mathcal{F}_{N}.



For the flip rates cx​(η)c_{x}(\eta) defined on 𝒳\mathcal{X}, we assume the
non-negativity, the locality (with the same range rc>0r_{c}>0 as cx,yc_{x,y})
and the spatial homogeneity, that is,
cx​(η)=τx​c​(η)c_{x}(\eta)=\tau_{x}c(\eta) for some local function c⁡(η)=c0​(η)≥0c(\eta)=c_{0}(\eta)\geq 0
on 𝒳\mathcal{X} (regarded as that on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} for NN large enough).
Since η0\eta_{0} takes values only in {0,1}\{0,1\}, c⁡(η)c(\eta) can be decomposed as




(1.3)

c⁡(η)=c+​(η)​(1−η0)+c−​(η)​η0c(\eta)=c^{+}(\eta)(1-\eta_{0})+c^{-}(\eta)\eta_{0}



for some local functions c±​(η)c^{\pm}(\eta) which do not depend on η0\eta_{0}.
We interpret c+​(η)c^{+}(\eta) and c−​(η)c^{-}(\eta) as the rates of creation and annihilation
of a particle at x=0x=0, respectively.




Let ηN​(t)={ηxN​(t);x∈𝕋Nd}\eta^{N}(t)=\{\eta_{x}^{N}(t);x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}\} be the Markov process
on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} governed by the infinitesimal generator ℒN\mathcal{L}_{N} in
(1.1) with properly taken KK such that
1≤K=K⁡(N)↗∞1\leq K=K(N)\nearrow\infty as N→∞N\to\infty.
We are interested in the asymptotic behavior as N→∞N\to\infty of
its macroscopic empirical mass distribution, that is,
the measure-valued process defined by




(1.4)

ρN​(t,d​v)=N−d​∑x∈𝕋NdηxN​(t)​δx/N​(d​v),v∈𝕋d,\rho^{N}(t,dv)=N^{-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\eta_{x}^{N}(t)\delta_{x/N}(dv),\quad v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},



where 𝕋d=ℝd/ℤd{\mathbb{T}}^{d}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}/{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} is a dd-dimensional continuous torus identified
with [0,1)d[0,1)^{d} and δv\delta_{v} is the δ\delta-measure at vv.




Our main result can be stated as follows. We consider the situation, under a proper
choice of rates {cx,y,cx}\{c_{x,y},c_{x}\}, that the particles favor two levels of mean
densities ρ+\rho_{+} and ρ−∈(0,1)\rho_{-}\in(0,1) with the same degree of stability.
We then prove that ρN​(t,d​v)\rho^{N}(t,dv) converges to ρ+​d​v\rho_{+}dv or ρ−​d​v\rho_{-}dv
on two regions separated by a hypersurface Γt\Gamma_{t} called the interface and Γt\Gamma_{t}
evolves under the anisotropic curvature flow; see Theorem 1.4.
We also give the rate of convergence in Theorem 1.1.




The proof is divided into two parts, that is, a probabilistic part
(Theorem 1.1) which is developed in this article and a PDE part
summarized in Theorems 1.2 and 1.3, shown in [17].





1.2 Diffusion matrix and reaction term at macroscopic level




To state our result for the hydrodynamic limit, which is the main contribution of
this article, first corresponding to the
Kawasaki part, we introduce a quadratic form
(θ,c^​(ρ)​θ),θ∈ℝd,\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho)\theta\right),\,\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},
for each ρ∈[0,1]\rho\in[0,1] called the conductivity via the variational formula




(1.5)

(θ,c^​(ρ)​θ)=infF∈ℱ0d(θ,c^​(ρ,F)​θ),\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho)\theta\right)=\inf_{F\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}}\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho;F)\theta\right),



where (⋅,⋅)(\cdot,\cdot) is the inner product of ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d},
ℱ0\mathcal{F}_{0} denotes the class of all
local functions on 𝒳\mathcal{X}, ℱ0d=(ℱ0)d\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}=(\mathcal{F}_{0})^{d},




(1.6)

(θ,c^​(ρ,F)​θ)=12​∑|x|=1⟨c0,x​(θ,x⁡(ηx−η0)−π0,x​(∑y∈ℤdτy​F))2⟩ρ,\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho;F)\theta\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|x|=1}\left\langle c_{0,x}\bigg(\theta,x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})-\pi_{0,x}\Big(\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\tau_{y}F\Big)\bigg)^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho},



and ⟨⋅⟩ρ≡Eνρ​[⋅]\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\rho}\equiv E^{\nu_{\rho}}[\,\cdot\,] stands for
the expectation with respect to the Bernoulli measure νρ\nu_{\rho} on
𝒳\mathcal{X}. Note that π0,x​(∑y∈ℤdτy​F)\pi_{0,x}(\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\,\tau_{y}F) is well-defined
as a finite sum ∑y∈ℤdπ0,x​(τy​F)\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\,\pi_{0,x}(\tau_{y}F).
We choose a d×dd\times d symmetric matrix
c^​(ρ)={c^i​j​(ρ)}1≤i,j≤d\widehat{c}(\rho)=\{\widehat{c}_{ij}(\rho)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq d},
which is written in the same notation, corresponding to the quadratic
form introduced above, especially to apply results in partial differential equations;
cf. Remark after Theorem 1.1 of [19].
Proposition 5.4, taken from [14], provides an information
on a minimizing sequence {Φn}n\{\Phi_{n}\}_{n} for FF in the variational formula
(1.5).




We also introduce the compressibility:




(1.7)

χ⁡(ρ)=ρ−ρ2,\chi(\rho)=\rho-\rho^{2},



and the diffusion matrix D⁡(ρ)={Di​j​(ρ)}1≤i,j≤dD(\rho)=\{D_{ij}(\rho)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq d} by the
Einstein relation:




(1.8)

D⁡(ρ)=c^​(ρ)2​χ​(ρ),ρ∈[0,1],D(\rho)=\frac{\widehat{c}(\rho)}{2\chi(\rho)},\quad\rho\in[0,1],



see Proposition 2.2 of [31], p. ​180 for the relation to the Green-Kubo formula.
It is known that D⁡(ρ)D(\rho) is a C∞C^{\infty}-function of
ρ∈[0,1]\rho\in[0,1]:




(1.9)

D∈C∞​([0,1]),D\in C^{\infty}([0,1]),



see [1].
Furthermore, the diffusion matrix D⁡(ρ)D(\rho) is uniformly positive and bounded:




(1.10)

c∗​|θ|2≤(θ,D⁡(ρ)​θ)≤c∗​|θ|2,θ∈ℝd,ρ∈[0,1],\displaystyle c_{*}|\theta|^{2}\leq(\theta,D(\rho)\theta)\leq c^{*}|\theta|^{2},\quad\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\;\rho\in[0,1],



where c∗,c∗>0c_{*},c^{*}>0 are constants defined by





0<c∗:=minη∈𝒳,x∈ℤd:|x|=1c0,x(η)≤c∗:=maxη∈𝒳,x∈ℤd:|x|=1c0,x(η)<∞,0<c_{*}:=\min_{\eta\in\mathcal{X},x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}:|x|=1}c_{0,x}(\eta)\leq c^{*}:=\max_{\eta\in\mathcal{X},x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}:|x|=1}c_{0,x}(\eta)<\infty,



which follows from the condition (1); see Lemma 12.1 below
for the proof of (1.10).




Next, corresponding to the Glauber part, we introduce a function f=f⁡(ρ)f=f(\rho)
as an ensemble average under νρ\nu_{\rho} as follows:




(1.11)

f⁡(ρ)\displaystyle f(\rho)
=⟨(1−2​η0)​c​(η)⟩ρ\displaystyle=\langle(1-2\eta_{0})c(\eta)\rangle_{\rho}





=(1−ρ)​⟨c+⟩ρ−ρ​⟨c−⟩ρ.\displaystyle=(1-\rho)\langle c^{+}\rangle_{\rho}-\rho\langle c^{-}\rangle_{\rho}.



Note that ff is a polynomial of ρ\rho and, in particular, a C∞C^{\infty}-function
of ρ∈[0,1]\rho\in[0,1]. Note also that f⁡(0)>0f(0)>0 and f⁡(1)<0f(1)<0, which is important to
show the comparison theorem; see Section 11.





1.3 Quantitative and non-gradient hydrodynamic limit




We show that, asymptotically as N→∞N\to\infty,
the macroscopic empirical mass distribution ρN​(t,d​v)\rho^{N}(t,dv)
is close to the solution ρ⁡(t,v)=ρK​(t,v)\rho(t,v)=\rho_{K}(t,v) of the following
reaction-diffusion equation with a nonlinear diffusion term and
a diverging factor K=K⁡(N)K=K(N) in a reaction term




(1.12)

∂tρ⁡(t,v)=∑i,j=1d∂vi{Di​j​(ρ⁡(t,v))​∂vjρ⁡(t,v)}+K​f​(ρ⁡(t,v)),t≥0,v∈𝕋d,\partial_{t}\rho(t,v)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\big\{D_{ij}(\rho(t,v))\partial_{v_{j}}\rho(t,v)\big\}+Kf(\rho(t,v)),\quad t\geq 0,\;v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},



where v=(vi)i=1dv=(v_{i})_{i=1}^{d}.
We assume that its initial value ρ0​(v)\rho_{0}(v) satisfies
the condition:




(1.13)

ρ0∈C5​(𝕋d) and 0<ρ0​(v)<1.\rho_{0}\in C^{5}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})\quad\text{ and }\quad 0<\rho_{0}(v)<1.



Then, the equation (1.12) has a unique classical solution ρ⁡(t,v)∈C1,3​([0,∞)×𝕋d)\rho(t,v)\in C^{1,3}([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}) by applying the results in [26];
see the beginning of Section 10.




To state our result, let νN≡ν1/2N\nu^{N}\equiv\nu_{1/2}^{N} be the Bernoulli measure
on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} with ρ=1/2\rho=1/2, that is, the uniform probability measure on
𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N}: νN​(η)=2−N,η∈𝒳N\nu^{N}(\eta)=2^{-N},\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}.
For two probability densities ff and gg with respect to νN\nu^{N},
define the relative entropy H⁡(f|g)≡HN​(f|g)H(f|g)\equiv H_{N}(f|g) by




(1.14)

H⁡(f|g)=∫𝒳Nf​log⁡(f/g)​d​νN.H(f|g)=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{N}}f\log(f/g)\,d\nu^{N}.



We set




(1.15)

ρ¯​(λ)=eλ/(eλ+1),λ∈ℝ,\bar{\rho}(\lambda)=e^{\lambda}/(e^{\lambda}+1),\quad\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}},



and denote its inverse function by
λ¯​(ρ)\bar{\lambda}(\rho):




(1.16)

λ¯​(ρ)=log⁡{ρ/(1−ρ)},ρ∈(0,1).\bar{\lambda}(\rho)=\log\{\rho/(1-\rho)\},\quad\rho\in(0,1).






Let f0=f0​(η),η∈𝒳N,f_{0}=f_{0}(\eta),\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}, be the (initial) density of the
distribution of ηN​(0)\eta^{N}(0) on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} with respect to νN\nu^{N}
and, for ρ0=ρ0​(v)\rho_{0}=\rho_{0}(v) satisfying (1.13), let




(1.17)

ψ0,0​(η)=ZN−1​exp⁡{∑x∈𝕋Ndλ¯​(ρ0​(x/N))​ηx}\psi_{0,0}(\eta)=Z_{N}^{-1}\exp\bigg\{\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\bar{\lambda}(\rho_{0}(x/N))\eta_{x}\bigg\}



with λ¯\bar{\lambda} defined by (1.16) and a normalization constant ZNZ_{N}
with respect to νN\nu^{N}. In other words, ψ0,0​d​νN\psi_{0,0}d\nu^{N}, denoted
also by Pψ0,0P^{\psi_{0,0}}, is a product measure on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} with
a marginal distribution Pψ0,0​(ηx=1)=ρ0​(x/N)P^{\psi_{0,0}}(\eta_{x}=1)=\rho_{0}(x/N) for
every x∈𝕋Ndx\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}. We use the nation ψ0,0\psi_{0,0} to be consistent with
ψ0,F\psi_{0,F} which denotes the local equilibrium state of the second order
approximation FF; cf. (2.1) and above Lemma 2.2.
In ψ0,0\psi_{0,0}, the first ‘00’ means ‘time 00’ and the second ‘00’
is for ‘F=0F=0’.




Then, the difference between ρN​(t,d​v)\rho^{N}(t,dv) and ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) with
K=K⁡(N)K=K(N) is estimated in L2​(Ω,H−α​(𝕋d))L^{2}(\Omega,H^{-\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}))
as in the following theorem. We call it a quantitative
hydrodynamic limit, since it gives the convergence rate. Note that
ρK\rho_{K} is also moving in NN. To set the upper threshold for the allowed
K=K⁡(N)K=K(N), define




(1.18)

K¯​(N)≡K¯δ​(N):=δ​log⁡N,\displaystyle\overline{K}(N)\equiv\overline{K}_{\delta}(N):=\delta\log N,



for δ>0\delta>0.



Theorem 1.1.



For each K≥1K\geq 1, let ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) be the solution of the equation (1.12)
with an initial value ρ0​(v)\rho_{0}(v) that satisfies the condition (1.13).
We assume that f0f_{0} and ψ0,0\psi_{0,0} defined above satisfy
H⁡(f0|ψ0,0)≤C1​Nd−κ1H(f_{0}|\psi_{0,0})\leq C_{1}N^{d-\kappa_{1}} for some C1>0C_{1}>0 and κ1>0\kappa_{1}>0.
Then, there exists 𝔠>0\mathfrak{c}>0 small enough such that
for any T>0T>0, if the sequence K=K⁡(N)→∞K=K(N)\to\infty (as N→∞N\to\infty)
satisfies 1≤K⁡(N)≤K¯δ​(N)1\leq K(N)\leq\overline{K}_{\delta}(N) with δ=δT:=𝔠/T\delta=\delta_{T}:=\mathfrak{c}/T,
we have




(1.19)

supt∈[0,T]E⁡[‖ρN​(t)−ρK​(t)‖H−α​(𝕋d)2]≤C​N−κ\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}E\big[\|\rho^{N}(t)-\rho_{K}(t)\|_{H^{-\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}^{2}\big]\leq CN^{-\kappa}



for every α>d/2\alpha>d/2, and some C=CT,α>0C=C_{T,\alpha}>0 and κ=κ𝔠,α>0\kappa=\kappa_{\mathfrak{c},\alpha}>0,
where H−α​(𝕋d)H^{-\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) is the usual Sobolev space on 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
with the exponent −α-\alpha; see Section 2.2 for the precise definition.





Dizdar et al. [7] has a similar quantitative result for
one-dimensional Ginzburg-Landau lattice model of gradient type with
d=1,α=1,κ=2/3d=1,\alpha=1,\kappa=2/3. In the above theorem, the range of α\alpha is α>d/2\alpha>d/2,
which is taken as widely as possible for the measure ρN​(t)\rho^{N}(t) on 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.
Our κ>0\kappa>0
is determined from several factors and small. The central limit theorem
scaling may suggest κ≃d\kappa\simeq d.




The constants 𝔠>0\mathfrak{c}>0 and δ>0\delta>0 in K¯δ​(N)\overline{K}_{\delta}(N)
are chosen in Theorem 2.1, or in Section 7.2,
for showing the estimate on the relative entropy. In fact, to make κ>0\kappa>0,
the product δ⋅T\delta\cdot T must be small enough and this determines 𝔠>0\mathfrak{c}>0.
Once 𝔠\mathfrak{c} is determined, we choose δ=δT=𝔠/T\delta=\delta_{T}=\mathfrak{c}/T. In this sense,
κ\kappa depends on 𝔠\mathfrak{c}, but it is independent of δ\delta and TT.
Then κ>0\kappa>0 in (1.19) is chosen
again depending on α\alpha in Section 2.2.




The assumption for H⁡(f0|ψ0,0)H(f_{0}|\psi_{0,0}) in this theorem is satisfied
if we take f0=ψ0,0f_{0}=\psi_{0,0}.




In [8], [15], a similar theorem
was shown for models of gradient type; see also [16].
In particular, it was shown that the theorem holds taking
K¯​(N)=δ​(log⁡N)σ/2\overline{K}(N)=\delta(\log N)^{\sigma/2} for some small δ>0\delta>0 and some
σ∈(0,1)\sigma\in(0,1). The bound for K¯​(N)\overline{K}(N) in Theorem 1.1
is better than this. The reason is that our estimates are more accurate
for several error terms. Moreover, for our non-gradient model,
new results obtained in [14] based on
the method of the quantitative homogenization theory are essential to derive
the above decay rate C​N−κCN^{-\kappa} and also to
determine the upper bound K¯​(N)\overline{K}(N).




Theorem 1.1 reduces the study of the limit of ρN​(t,d​v)\rho^{N}(t,dv) as
N→∞N\to\infty to that of ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) as K→∞K\to\infty.
The latter is a pure PDE problem and is discussed separately in a companion
paper [17]. The results are summarized in the next subsection.





1.4 Interface motion from nonlinear Allen-Cahn equation




We now consider the case that d≥2d\geq 2 and assume that the reaction term
f⁡(ρ)f(\rho) in (1.12) is bistable,
i.e. ff has three zeros 0<ρ−<ρ∗<ρ+<10<\rho_{-}<\rho_{*}<\rho_{+}<1 such that
f′​(ρ±)<0f^{\prime}(\rho_{\pm})<0 and f′​(ρ∗)>0f^{\prime}(\rho_{*})>0.
In addition, we assume the balance condition:





∫ρ−ρ+f⁡(ρ)​D​(ρ)​𝑑ρ=0,\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}f(\rho)D(\rho)d\rho=0,



as matrices.
This means that two stable phases with densities ρ±\rho_{\pm},
sparse and dense, have the same degree of stability.
See Section 13 for some examples of the jump rate cx,y​(η)c_{x,y}(\eta)
of the Kawasaki part and the flip rate cx​(η)c_{x}(\eta) of the Glauber part
for which the corresponding ff and DD satisfy all these conditions.
Recalling the condition (1.13) for the initial value ρ0​(v)\rho_{0}(v),
we define Γ0\Gamma_{0} by





Γ0:={v∈𝕋d:ρ0​(v)=ρ∗}\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}:=\{v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}:\rho_{0}(v)=\rho_{*}\}



and assume that Γ0\Gamma_{0} is a C5C^{5}-hypersurface in 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d} without boundary
such that




(1.20)

∇ρ0​(v)⋅n⁡(v)≠0​ for ​v∈Γ0,\displaystyle\nabla\rho_{0}(v)\cdot n(v)\neq 0~\text{ for }~v\in\Gamma_{0},



where n⁡(v)n(v) is the normal vector to Γ0\Gamma_{0}.
Two regions Ω0±\Omega_{0}^{\pm} surrounded by Γ0\Gamma_{0} are defined by
Ω0+={ρ0>ρ∗}\Omega_{0}^{+}=\{\rho_{0}>\rho_{*}\} and Ω0−={ρ0<ρ∗}\Omega_{0}^{-}=\{\rho_{0}<\rho_{*}\}, respectively.




Then, one can show that





ρK​(t,v)→ΞΓt​(v)as ​K→∞.\rho_{K}(t,v)\to\Xi_{\Gamma_{t}}(v)\quad\text{as }\;K\to\infty.



Here, Γt\Gamma_{t} is a smooth closed hypersurface in 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d} and ΞΓt\Xi_{\Gamma_{t}}
is a step function taking two values ρ±\rho_{\pm} defined by





ΞΓt(v)={ρ+,v∈Ωt+,ρ−,v∈Ωt−,\Xi_{\Gamma_{t}}(v)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \rho_{+},&\quad v\in\Omega_{t}^{+},\\
\rho_{-},&\quad v\in\Omega_{t}^{-},\end{aligned}\right.



where Ωt±\Omega_{t}^{\pm} are two regions surrounded by Γt\Gamma_{t}.
The sides of these regions are determined initially at t=0t=0 and
then continuously for t>0t>0.




The evolution of Γt\Gamma_{t} starting from Γ0\Gamma_{0} is governed by the equation




(1.21)

V=−Tr(μ(n)∂vn)≡−∑i,j=1dμi​j(n)∂vinjon Γt,V=-{\rm Tr}(\mu(n)\partial_{v}n)\equiv-\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\mu_{ij}(n)\partial_{v_{i}}n_{j}\quad\text{on }~\Gamma_{t},



where VV is the normal velocity of Γt\Gamma_{t} from the side of Ωt−\Omega_{t}^{-} to
Ωt+\Omega_{t}^{+}
and n=(ni)n=(n_{i}) denotes the unit normal vector to Γt\Gamma_{t} of the same direction.
This describes an anisotropic (direction dependent) curvature flow.
It is a mean curvature flow in the special case that μi​j​(e)=μ​δi​j\mu_{ij}(e)=\mu\delta_{ij}
with a constant μ\mu.




The matrix μ⁡(e)={μi​j​(e)}1≤i,j≤d\mu(e)=\{\mu_{ij}(e)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq d} is defined
for e∈ℝde\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}: |e|=1|e|=1 as





μi​j​(e)\displaystyle\mu_{ij}(e)
=1λ⁡(e)​∫ρ−ρ+[Di​j​(ρ)​We​(ρ)−12​∂ei(We​(ρ))​∂ej(ae​(ρ)We​(ρ))]​𝑑ρ,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda(e)}\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}\left[D_{ij}(\rho)\sqrt{W_{e}(\rho)}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{e_{i}}(W_{e}(\rho))\,\partial_{e_{j}}\left(\frac{a_{e}(\rho)}{\sqrt{W_{e}(\rho)}}\right)\right]d\rho,




λ⁡(e)\displaystyle\lambda(e)
=∫ρ−ρ+We​(ρ)dρ,We(ρ)=−2∫ρ−ρae(ρ)f(ρ)dρ,ae(ρ)=e⋅D(ρ)e.\displaystyle=\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}\sqrt{W_{e}(\rho)}d\rho,\quad W_{e}(\rho)=-2\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho}a_{e}(\rho)f(\rho)d\rho,\quad a_{e}(\rho)=e\cdot D(\rho)e.



Note that We​(ρ)≥0W_{e}(\rho)\geq 0 for ρ∈[ρ−,ρ+]\rho\in[\rho_{-},\rho_{+}] by the conditions
for f⁡(ρ)f(\rho).




The local-in-time well-posedness on a certain time interval [0,T][0,T] with
T>0T>0 of the equation (1.21) follows from the non-degeneracy of μ⁡(e)\mu(e)
in the tangential direction to the interface Γ\Gamma: For some c>0c>0,





(θ,μ⁡(e)​θ)≥c​|θ|2 for ​θ∈ℝd​ such that ​(θ,e)=0.(\theta,\mu(e)\theta)\geq c|\theta|^{2}\quad\text{ for }\;\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\;\text{ such that }\;(\theta,e)=0.



Note that (1.21) can be rewritten in an equivalent PDE for the signed
distance function d⁡(t,v)d(t,v) from Γt\Gamma_{t}; see [17].




The first result is for the generation of the interface, that is,
ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) reaches the neighborhood of ρ−\rho_{-} or ρ+\rho_{+} in
a very short time of order K−1​log⁡KK^{-1}\log K.



Theorem 1.2.



(Theorem 1.1 of [17] on Ω=𝕋d\Omega={\mathbb{T}}^{d})
Let ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) be the solution of the equation (1.12) and let
ϵ\epsilon be such that 0<ϵ<ρ¯0<\epsilon<\bar{\rho}, where ρ¯:=min⁡{ρ+−ρ∗,ρ∗−ρ−}\bar{\rho}:=\min\{\rho_{+}-\rho_{*},\rho_{*}-\rho_{-}\}. We assume the conditions
given at the beginning of this subsection.
Then, there exist K0>0K_{0}>0 and M0>0M_{0}>0 such that, for all
K≥K0K\geq K_{0}, the following holds at t=tK:=K−1​log⁡K/(2​f′​(ρ∗))t=t_{K}:=K^{-1}\log K/(2f^{\prime}(\rho_{*})).


(1)(1) 



ρ−−ϵ≤ρK​(tK,v)≤ρ++ϵ\rho_{-}-\epsilon\leq\rho_{K}(t_{K},v)\leq\rho_{+}+\epsilon for all v∈𝕋dv\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},


(2)(2) 



ρK​(tK,v)≥ρ+−ϵ\rho_{K}(t_{K},v)\geq\rho_{+}-\epsilon
for v∈𝕋dv\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d} such that ρ0​(v)≥ρ∗+M0/K1/2\rho_{0}(v)\geq\rho_{*}+M_{0}/K^{1/2},


(3)(3) 



ρK​(tK,v)≤ρ−+ϵ\rho_{K}(t_{K},v)\leq\rho_{-}+\epsilon
for v∈𝕋dv\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d} such that ρ0​(v)≤ρ∗−M0/K1/2\rho_{0}(v)\leq\rho_{*}-M_{0}/K^{1/2}.







The second result is for the propagation of the interface, that is,
the derivation of the interface motion Γt\Gamma_{t} as long as it remains smooth.
The size of the transition layer is O(K−1/2)O(K^{-1/2}).



Theorem 1.3.



(Theorem 1.2 of [17])
Under the conditions of Theorem 1.2, for any 0<ϵ<ρ¯0<\epsilon<\bar{\rho}, there exist K0>0,C>0K_{0}>0,C>0 and T>0T>0 (within the
local-in-time well-posedness of (1.21))
such that for every K≥K0K\geq K_{0}
and t∈[tK,T]t\in[t_{K},T] we have





ρK​(t,v)∈{[ρ−−ϵ,ρ++ϵ] for all ​v∈𝕋d,[ρ+−ϵ,ρ++ϵ] if ​v∈Ωt+∖𝒩C/K1/2​(Γt),[ρ−−ϵ,ρ−+ϵ] if ​v∈Ωt−∖𝒩C/K1/2​(Γt),\displaystyle\rho_{K}(t,v)\in~\begin{cases}[\rho_{-}-\epsilon,\rho_{+}+\epsilon]&\text{ for all }v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},\\
[\rho_{+}-\epsilon,\rho_{+}+\epsilon]&\text{ if }v\in\Omega_{t}^{+}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{C/K^{1/2}}(\Gamma_{t}),\\
[\rho_{-}-\epsilon,\rho_{-}+\epsilon]&\text{ if }v\in\Omega_{t}^{-}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{C/K^{1/2}}(\Gamma_{t}),\end{cases}



where 𝒩r​(Γt):={v∈𝕋d,dist⁡(v,Γt)≤r}\mathcal{N}_{r}(\Gamma_{t}):=\{v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},{\rm dist}(v,\Gamma_{t})\leq r\} is the
rr-neighborhood of Γt\Gamma_{t}.





The proofs of these two theorems are given based on the comparison theorem
for the PDE (1.12). We construct its super and sub solutions.
If the PDE (1.12) is a gradient flow
of a certain functional, one can apply the method of Γ\Gamma-convergence
to derive the motion of Γt\Gamma_{t}. In particular, if the energy in the limit
is a total surface tension, one would obtain an evolution of Wulff shape.
But our equation is different from this class.





1.5 Main result




The following theorem is obtained for the particle system ηN​(t)\eta^{N}(t)
(with K=K⁡(N)K=K(N))
by combining Theorems 1.1 and 1.3.
The convergence rate for (1.22) is much slower than N−κN^{-\kappa}
given in Theorem 1.1, since it contains (1.23)
and the rate for this term is given in terms of KK.



Theorem 1.4.



Assume the condition (1.13) for the initial value ρ0​(v)\rho_{0}(v) and
H⁡(f0|ψ0,0)≤C1​Nd−κ1H(f_{0}|\psi_{0,0})\leq C_{1}N^{d-\kappa_{1}} for some C1>0C_{1}>0 and κ1>0\kappa_{1}>0
in Theorem 1.1 and
those in Theorem 1.3, that is, d≥2d\geq 2, f⁡(ρ)f(\rho) determined
in (1.11) from the flip rate of the Glauber part and the initial value
ρ0​(v)\rho_{0}(v) satisfy the conditions given at the beginning of Section 1.4.
We also assume the sequence K⁡(N)→∞K(N)\to\infty satisfies 1≤K⁡(N)≤K¯δT​(N)1\leq K(N)\leq\overline{K}_{\delta_{T}}(N) as in Theorem 1.1.




Then, for every α>d/2\alpha>d/2 and t0∈(0,T]t_{0}\in(0,T], we have




(1.22)

limN→∞supt∈[t0,T]E⁡[‖ρN​(t)−ΞΓt‖H−α​(𝕋d)2]=0,\lim_{N\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T]}E[\|\rho^{N}(t)-\Xi_{\Gamma_{t}}\|_{H^{-\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}^{2}]=0,



where T>0T>0 is determined as in Theorem 1.3.




Proof.



Noting that ΞΓt=ρ+​1Ωt++ρ−​1Ωt−\Xi_{\Gamma_{t}}=\rho_{+}1_{\Omega_{t}^{+}}+\rho_{-}1_{\Omega_{t}^{-}} and |𝕋d|=1|{\mathbb{T}}^{d}|=1,
for any 0<ϵ<ρ¯0<\epsilon<\bar{\rho}, we have by Theorem 1.3





|⟨ρK​(t),ϕ⟩−⟨ΞΓt,ϕ⟩|≤(ϵ+(Cϵ/K1/2)d−1⋅|Γt|)​‖ϕ‖∞|\langle\rho_{K}(t),\phi\rangle-\langle\Xi_{\Gamma_{t}},\phi\rangle|\leq\big(\epsilon+(C_{\epsilon}/K^{1/2})^{d-1}\cdot|\Gamma_{t}|\big)\|\phi\|_{\infty}



for K≥K0=K0,ϵK\geq K_{0}=K_{0,\epsilon} and t∈[tK,T]t\in[t_{K},T]. This implies




(1.23)

‖ρK​(t)−ΞΓt‖H−α​(𝕋d)≤C⁡(ϵ+(Cϵ/K1/2)d−1⋅|Γt|)\|\rho_{K}(t)-\Xi_{\Gamma_{t}}\|_{H^{-\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\leq C\big(\epsilon+(C_{\epsilon}/K^{1/2})^{d-1}\cdot|\Gamma_{t}|\big)



for α>d/2\alpha>d/2 and some C>0C>0 as in the proof of Theorem 1.1
given in Section 2.2. Choose ϵ>0\epsilon>0
small enough and K=K⁡(N)≥K0K=K(N)\geq K_{0} taking NN large. Then, since
|Γt||\Gamma_{t}| is bounded, one can make the right-hand side smaller than
2​C​ϵ2C\epsilon for large enough NN. Note also that tK≤t0t_{K}\leq t_{0} holds
for KK large. Thus, the conclusion follows from Theorem 1.1.
∎





This theorem establishes the autonomous phase separation directly for
the particle system. The PDE (1.12) was used only secondarily.
We set the flip rate c0c_{0} in the Glauber part as the corresponding ff
determined in (1.11) to satisfy the bistability and balance conditions.
This means that the particles prefer two phases with mean densities
ρ−\rho_{-} or ρ+\rho_{+}.




From gradient models, we derived the mean curvature flow, i.e.,
μi​j​(e)=μ​δi​j\mu_{ij}(e)=\mu\delta_{ij}; see [8], [9],
[15] and also [23], [3].
In the unbalanced case for gradient models
assuming that D⁡(ρ)≡DD(\rho)\equiv D (constant),
on a shorter time scale such as O⁡(1/K1/2)O(1/K^{1/2}),
Huygens’ principle was derived. In other words, the stronger phase region expands
with a constant speed; see [16]. The emergence of phase-separating
interface continuum flows in large scale interacting particle systems has been of
long-standing interest; see [6], [31].
See [12] for other approaches.





1.6 Outline of the article




The article is organized as follows. In Section 2, following
[19], we introduce a local equilibrium state ψt​(η)\psi_{t}(\eta) of second
order approximation with a leading order term determined by the hydrodynamic
equation (1.12). Then we prove that Theorem 1.1 is shown
once one can prove the bound N−d​H​(ft|ψt)≤C​N−κN^{-d}H(f_{t}|\psi_{t})\leq CN^{-\kappa}, t≥0t\geq 0 for some
C,κ>0C,\kappa>0 for the relative entropy per volume of the density ft​(η)=ftN​(η)f_{t}(\eta)=f_{t}^{N}(\eta) of
the distribution of the process ηN​(t)\eta^{N}(t) with respect to ψt​(η)\psi_{t}(\eta)
with a properly chosen second order term F⁡(η)F(\eta).
This bound is formulated in Theorem 2.1.




Thus, our goal is to prove Theorem 2.1, i.e. the entropy bound
H⁡(ft|ψt)≤C​Nd−κH(f_{t}|\psi_{t})\leq CN^{d-\kappa}. We start the proof in Section 3.
It is concluded in Section 8.
Unlike in [19], our model has the Glauber part and it
includes a diverging factor K=K⁡(N)K=K(N). Moreover, in order to establish
a quantitative hydrodynamic limit, we need to derive fine
error estimates at every step of the proof.
The proof of Theorem 2.1 is outlined in Section 2.3
to clarify the main steps of the arguments.
It is also provided here below.




In Section 3,
we first calculate the time derivative of H⁡(ft|ψt)H(f_{t}|\psi_{t}) for a general ψt\psi_{t}
along with careful error estimates; see Lemma 3.1.
Then, we formulate the refined one-block estimate,
that is, the replacement of microscopic functions (even those with a diverging
factor KK) by their ensemble averages, providing fine error estimates;
see Proposition 3.2.




Sections 4 and 5 are the core of the non-gradient and
quantitative method.
In Section 4, we show the Boltzmann-Gibbs principle for
the gradient replacement, by which one can replace a microscopic function
that looks of diverging
order O⁡(N)O(N) but with vanishing ensemble average by a linear function of ηx\eta_{x}
of gradient form with a properly chosen coefficient; see Theorem 4.1.
This theorem, which gives good error estimates for the replacement, is proven
by using a key lemma stated as Lemma 4.2
(sometimes called Kipnis-Varadhan estimate or Itô-Tanaka trick) and
the decay estimates for the so-called CLT (central limit theorem)
variances given in Section
5. We then need Lemma 4.3 to replace the linear function
of ηx\eta_{x} of gradient form with a function of order O⁡(1)O(1).




Section 5 concerns the CLT variances related to the gradient
replacement and deals with quantities determined only by the localized Kawasaki
generator and under the Bernoulli measures {νρ}ρ\{\nu_{\rho}\}_{\rho}.
In particular, the Glauber part plays no role in this section.
For the decay rate for the CLT variance of the term AℓA_{\ell} (a linear function
of ηx\eta_{x} of gradient form), we have Theorem 5.1
as a refinement of Theorem 5.1 of [19].
This theorem and Proposition 5.4, which provides a decay estimate
for a minimizing sequence for the variational formula (1.5),
are the key in the quantitative gradient replacement and are obtained by
applying new results from [14].
We note that Theorem 5.1 of [19]
gave only the convergence without rate and it was shown based on the so-called
characterization of the closed forms originally due to Varadhan [34];
cf. [25]. However, this is not sufficient for our purpose. Instead, we use
the results of [14], which were shown inspired by the recent progress
in quantitative homogenization theory. Proposition 5.2,
which calculates the CLT variances for other terms, is
a refinement of Proposition 5.1 of [19].
The results of this section are used in the proof of Theorem 4.1.




In Section 6, we first summarize
the estimates obtained so far in Lemma 6.1.
To provide a further bound for an expectation under ftf_{t} (which is an unknown
distribution) by that under ψt\psi_{t} (which is a well-understood distribution),
we apply the entropy inequality. Next, we are required to show the large
deviation type upper bound under ψt\psi_{t} with fine error estimates (see
Lemma 6.2) and, as its consequence, we obtain
Proposition 6.3.
One can observe that the main term in the estimate in Proposition
6.3 is negligible when the leading term of ψt\psi_{t} is determined
according to the hydrodynamic equation (1.12); see Lemma 6.4.
The equation (1.12) is used only for this lemma.




In Section 7, the choices of the second order term FN=Φn⁡(N)F_{N}=\Phi_{n(N)} of ψt\psi_{t} and the mesoscopic scaling parameters n=n⁡(N)n=n(N) and
ℓ=ℓ⁡(N)\ell=\ell(N) are given. We apply multi-scale analysis
with different scaling parameters chosen as 1≪n≪ℓ≪N1\ll n\ll\ell\ll N.
We use Proposition 5.4 to determine Φn\Phi_{n} for FNF_{N}.
We have the convergence rate of the diffusion matrix
approximated from the finite volume, which is shown to be uniform in the density,
and an L∞L^{\infty}-estimate on the local function Φn\Phi_{n} with support size nn,
which is a minimizing sequence for the variational formula (1.5) in FF.
The possible range of K=K⁡(N)K=K(N): K≤K¯δ​(N)=δ​log⁡NK\leq\overline{K}_{\delta}(N)=\delta\log N,
is determined in this section. The Schauder estimates for the solution of
the hydrodynamic equation (1.12) are used essentially only in this section.




In Section 8, we summarize all these results and conclude the
proof of Theorem 2.1.




Section 9 gathers the proofs of lemmas and propositions,
postponed from Sections 3–6:
Lemma 3.1 (calculation of ∂thN​(t)\partial_{t}h_{N}(t)),
Proposition 3.2 (refined one-block estimate),
Lemma 4.2 (a key lemma),
Lemma 4.3 (rewriting O⁡(N)O(N)-looking term to O⁡(1)O(1)),
Proposition 5.2 (CLT variance)
and Lemma 6.4 (showing g⁡(t)≤0g(t)\leq 0 for small δ>0\delta>0).
These are shown based on known results with some modifications
and refinements, providing careful error estimates. For example,
for Proposition 3.2, we combine
the argument in [15] and the equivalence of ensembles
with a precise convergence rate. For Proposition 5.2,
we provide a decay estimate for the error terms in the CLT variances,
which was not given in [19]. We also use the equivalence
of ensembles with a precise convergence rate.
Careful error estimates are found also in [22] for related models.




Sections 10, 11, 12 and 13 are
complementary and are devoted to the proofs, respectively, of
the Schauder estimates, the comparison
theorem, the non-degeneracy of the diffusion matrix D⁡(ρ)D(\rho) and to
giving examples of the rates {cx,y}\{c_{x,y}\} and {cx}\{c_{x}\} that satisfy
our assumptions for D⁡(ρ)D(\rho) and f⁡(ρ)f(\rho).




In Appendices, though they are well-known or shown by elementary calculations,
for the sake of completeness, we show the rr-Markov property used
in the proof of Lemma 3.1 in Appendix A and
the large deviation error estimate stated in Lemma 6.2
in Appendix B
for the local equilibrium state ψt\psi_{t} of second order approximation
defined by (2.1).





1.7 Discussion




The restriction K⁡(N)≤δ​log⁡NK(N)\leq\delta\log N for the diverging speed of K⁡(N)K(N)
is technical and caused when we apply Gronwall’s inequality at the end
of the proof in Section 8; see also (7.12).
This condition for K⁡(N)K(N) is certainly not the best. When the Kawasaki part
is the simple exclusion, [23] showed a similar result for
KK such that K⁡(N)=N2​ζ∗/(1+ζ∗)×o⁡(1)K(N)=N^{2\zeta^{*}/(1+\zeta^{*})}\times o(1) (as N→∞N\to\infty)
for a certain constant ζ∗∈(0,1)\zeta^{*}\in(0,1) and they derived the mean curvature flow
for Γt\Gamma_{t} globally in time interpreted in the viscosity sense
beyond the time that a geometric singularity appears in Γt\Gamma_{t}.
(Their scaling parameters ε\varepsilon and λ=λ⁡(ε)\lambda=\lambda(\varepsilon) are related to our
NN and K=K⁡(N)K=K(N) as ε=K1/2/N\varepsilon=K^{1/2}/N and λ=K−1/2\lambda=K^{-1/2}.)
Their method for the particle system seems difficult to apply to our model.




We consider the Kawasaki part which has the Bernoulli product measures
as its reversible measures. This corresponds to an infinite temperature model.
The hydrodynamic limit for the Kawasaki dynamics of non-gradient type,
which has the canonical Gibbs measures as its reversible measures, was
studied in [35]. It is natural to study our problem for such dynamics
added the Glauber part. To do this, we need to extend the results of
[14] to the Kawasaki dynamics associated with the canonical
Gibbs measures. Currently, this extension is an open and difficult problem.




The case of an unbalanced reaction term was studied in [16]
when the Kawasaki part is simple. It is natural to generalize this to the
non-gradient Kawasaki case, but this was nontrivial even for the gradient
Kawasaki part with a speed change. The reason is that we have a PDE
problem: we need to study the sharp interface limit for an unbalanced
reaction term and nonlinear diffusion. This remains unsolved.






2 Proof of Theorem 1.1




2.1 Formulation of Theorem 2.1: estimate on the
relative entropy




To prove Theorem 1.1, we apply the relative entropy
method as in [19] comparing the distribution of ηN​(t)\eta^{N}(t) with a
properly taken local equilibrium state
of second order approximation. Its leading term is
determined from the solution ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) of the hydrodynamic equation
(1.12). The second order term is determined by F=F⁡(η)F=F(\eta) which
appears in the variational formula (1.5), and plays a similar role to
the corrector in the theory of homogenization. Note that, for gradient
models, the second order approximation is unnecessary; see
[8], [15], [16].




Given a function λ=λ⁡(t,v)∈C1,3​([0,T]×𝕋d)\lambda=\lambda(t,v)\in C^{1,3}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d})
and a function F=F⁡(η)∈ℱ0dF=F(\eta)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}, we define a local equilibrium
state ψt​(η)​d​νN\psi_{t}(\eta)d\nu^{N} of second order approximation by




(2.1)

ψt​(η)\displaystyle\psi_{t}(\eta)
≡ψλ⁡(t,⋅),F​(η)\displaystyle\equiv\psi_{\lambda(t,\cdot),F}(\eta)





=Zt−1​exp⁡{∑x∈𝕋Ndλ⁡(t,x/N)​ηx+1N​∑x∈𝕋Nd(∂λ⁡(t,x/N),τx​F​(η))},\displaystyle=Z_{t}^{-1}\exp\bigg\{\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\lambda(t,x/N)\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\left(\partial\lambda(t,x/N),\tau_{x}F(\eta)\right)\bigg\},



for η∈𝒳N\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N},
where Zt=Zλ⁡(t,⋅),FZ_{t}=Z_{\lambda(t,\cdot),F} is the normalization constant
with respect to νN≡ν1/2N\nu^{N}\equiv\nu_{1/2}^{N} and ∂λ≡∂vλ={∂viλ}1≤i≤d\partial\lambda\equiv\partial_{v}\lambda=\{\partial_{v_{i}}\lambda\}_{1\leq i\leq d}.
We also write λ˙=∂λ/∂t\dot{\lambda}=\partial\lambda/\partial t, ∂2λ≡∂v2λ={∂vi∂vjλ}1≤i,j≤d\partial^{2}\lambda\equiv\partial_{v}^{2}\lambda=\left\{\partial_{v_{i}}\partial_{v_{j}}\lambda\right\}_{1\leq i,j\leq d} and

Denote by ft​(η)=ftN​(η)f_{t}(\eta)=f_{t}^{N}(\eta) the density of the

and consider the relative entropy per volume defined by

and κ>0\kappa>0 by choosing λ⁡(t,⋅)\lambda(t,\cdot) and FF in ψt=ψtN​(η)\psi_{t}=\psi_{t}^{N}(\eta)
properly as in the following theorem.



Theorem 2.1.

Then, there exist some

The proof of Theorem 2.1 will be given in Section 8
starting from Section 3. We will calculate the time derivative
of hN​(t)h_{N}(t), in which derivatives of λK​(t,v)\lambda_{K}(t,v) appear.
For the solution ρ=ρK​(t,v)\rho=\rho_{K}(t,v) of the hydrodynamic equation (1.12)
with smooth coefficients DD and K​fKf, we have the Schauder estimates
in terms of K≥1K\geq 1:




(2.3)

‖∂kρ‖∞,‖∂tρ‖∞,‖∂∂tρ‖∞≤C​Kα0,k=1,2,3,\|\partial^{k}\rho\|_{\infty},\;\|\partial_{t}\rho\|_{\infty},\;\|\partial\partial_{t}\rho\|_{\infty}\leq CK^{\alpha_{0}},\quad k=1,2,3,



for some α0>0\alpha_{0}>0 and C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0, where

for details. In particular, for λ=λK​(t,v)=λ¯​(ρK​(t,v))\lambda=\lambda_{K}(t,v)=\bar{\lambda}(\rho_{K}(t,v)),
recalling the definition (1.16) of λ¯\bar{\lambda} and noting that ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) is

(2.4)

‖∂λ‖3,∞,‖λ˙‖∞,‖∂λ˙‖∞≤C​Kα0\displaystyle\|\partial\lambda\|_{3,\infty},\;\|\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty},\;\|\partial\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty}\leq CK^{\alpha_{0}}



by changing C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0 (depending on ‖λ‖∞\|\lambda\|_{\infty}), where we set

To show (2.4) from (2.3),
note that for the function λ¯=λ¯​(ρ)\bar{\lambda}=\bar{\lambda}(\rho)
defined by (1.16) we have




(2.5)

∂λ¯∂ρ=1χ⁡(ρ),∂2λ¯∂ρ2=−1+2​ρχ​(ρ)2,∂3λ¯∂ρ3=2​(1−3​ρ+3​ρ2)χ​(ρ)3,\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bar{\lambda}}{\partial\rho}=\frac{1}{\chi(\rho)},\quad\frac{\partial^{2}\bar{\lambda}}{\partial\rho^{2}}=\frac{-1+2\rho}{\chi(\rho)^{2}},\quad\frac{\partial^{3}\bar{\lambda}}{\partial\rho^{3}}=\frac{2(1-3\rho+3\rho^{2})}{\chi(\rho)^{3}},



where χ⁡(ρ)=ρ⁡(1−ρ)\chi(\rho)=\rho(1-\rho) was introduced in (1.7).




The Schauder estimates (2.3), (2.4)
are actually used only in Section 7 except for (2.8)
and (2.16) below.
But, as these estimates suggest and also as eK​t/δ∗e^{Kt/\delta_{*}} appears in
(8.1) after
applying Gronwall’s inequality at the end of the proof in Section 8,
at all steps in the proof of Theorem
2.1, we need to obtain error estimates that are strong enough
to control diverging factors caused by KK. This is used to show
the quantitative version of the hydrodynamic limit as well.




Note that, as the hydrodynamic equation, a discrete PDE was used in
[8], [15], [16] to ensure an exact cancellation
for the leading term in the entropy computation, but here we use a continuous
PDE (1.12), since our
error estimates are strong enough to cover the difference.

Next, for β∈(0,1]\beta\in(0,1], let us consider the Hölder norms
‖g‖Cβ=‖g‖Cβ​(𝕋d)\|g\|_{C^{\beta}}=\|g\|_{C^{\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})} of a function gg on 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d}:

where |v|∈[0,d/2]|v|\in[0,\sqrt{d}/2] is the Euclidean norm
defined for v∈𝕋dv\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d} modulo 11 in each component.
Later, we will take ρK​(t,⋅)\rho_{K}(t,\cdot) for ρ⁡(⋅)\rho(\cdot) in the next lemma.



Lemma 2.3.



For every β∈(0,1]\beta\in(0,1] and ρ=ρ⁡(⋅)\rho=\rho(\cdot), ϕ=ϕ⁡(⋅)∈Cβ​(𝕋d)\phi=\phi(\cdot)\in C^{\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}),
we have

Proof.



The proof is easy:





|⟨ρ,ϕ⟩N−⟨ρ,ϕ⟩|\displaystyle|\langle\rho,\phi\rangle_{N}-\langle\rho,\phi\rangle|
=|∑x∈𝕋Nd∫B⁡(x/N,1/N)(ρ⁡(x/N)​ϕ​(x/N)−ρ⁡(v)​ϕ​(v))​𝑑v|\displaystyle=\bigg|\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\int_{B(x/N,1/N)}\Big(\rho(x/N)\phi(x/N)-\rho(v)\phi(v)\Big)dv\bigg|





≤∑x∈𝕋NdN−d​‖ρ⋅ϕ‖Cβ​(𝕋d)​supv∈B⁡(x/N,1/N)|v−x/N|β\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}N^{-d}\|\rho\cdot\phi\|_{C^{\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\sup_{v\in B(x/N,1/N)}\big|v-x/N\big|^{\beta}





≤(d/2​N)β​‖ρ⋅ϕ‖Cβ​(𝕋d),\displaystyle\leq\big(\sqrt{d}/2N\big)^{\beta}\|\rho\cdot\phi\|_{C^{\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})},



where B⁡(x/N,1/N)B(x/N,1/N) is the box in 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d} with center x/Nx/N and side length
1/N1/N.
∎





We are at the position to give the proof of Theorem 1.1.
For the sake of completeness,
let us define the Sobolev spaces Hα≡Hα​(𝕋d)H^{\alpha}\equiv H^{\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) on 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
for α∈ℝ\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}. We consider the Laplacian Δ\Delta on 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d}. Its
normalized eigenfunctions are products of





{2​sin⁡π​ni​vi,1,2​cos⁡π​mi​vi}ni,mi=1,2,…\{\sqrt{2}\sin\pi n_{i}v_{i},1,\sqrt{2}\cos\pi m_{i}v_{i}\}_{n_{i},m_{i}=1,2,\ldots}



choosing one for each i=1,2,…,di=1,2,\ldots,d. We denote them by e𝐧e_{\bf n}
for 𝐧=(n1,…,nd)∈ℤd{\bf n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}, i.e., we choose
2​sin⁡π​ni​vi\sqrt{2}\sin\pi n_{i}v_{i}, 11 or 2​cos⁡π⁡(−ni)​vi\sqrt{2}\cos\pi(-n_{i})v_{i}
for ii according to ni>0n_{i}>0, ni=0n_{i}=0 or ni<0n_{i}<0, respectively,
and make product to define e𝐧​(v),v∈𝕋de_{\bf n}(v),v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.
Then, e𝐧e_{\bf n} is an eigenfunction of −Δ-\Delta with
eigenvalue λ𝐧:=π2​|𝐧|2≡π2​∑i=1dni2\lambda_{\bf n}:=\pi^{2}|{\bf n}|^{2}\equiv\pi^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{d}n_{i}^{2},
i.e. −Δ​e𝐧=λ𝐧​e𝐧-\Delta e_{\bf n}=\lambda_{\bf n}e_{\bf n}. Moreover,
{e𝐧}𝐧∈ℤd\{e_{\bf n}\}_{{\bf n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}} forms a complete orthonormal system
of L2​(𝕋d)L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}).




Define the norm ‖g‖Hα\|g\|_{H^{\alpha}} first for g∈C∞​(𝕋d)g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) by




(2.12)

‖g‖Hα2:=⟨(−Δ+1)α​g,g⟩≡∑𝐧∈ℤd(λ𝐧+1)α​⟨g,e𝐧⟩2,\displaystyle\|g\|_{H^{\alpha}}^{2}:=\langle(-\Delta+1)^{\alpha}g,g\rangle\equiv\sum_{{\bf n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}(\lambda_{\bf n}+1)^{\alpha}\langle g,e_{\bf n}\rangle^{2},



where ⟨⋅,⋅⟩\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle denotes the inner product of L2​(𝕋d)L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}).
The Sobolev space Hα​(𝕋d)H^{\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) is the completion of C∞​(𝕋d)C^{\infty}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
under the norm ∥⋅∥Hα\|\cdot\|_{H^{\alpha}}. Note that discrete measures on 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
such as ρN​(t)\rho^{N}(t) are elements of H−α​(𝕋d)H^{-\alpha}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) for α>d/2\alpha>d/2,
but not for a≤d/2a\leq d/2 in general. For example, we easily see
‖δ0‖H−α=∞\|\delta_{0}\|_{H^{-\alpha}}=\infty for α≤d/2\alpha\leq d/2.



Proof of Theorem 1.1.



By (2.12), one can rewrite as

+2​(⟨ρK​(t),e𝐧⟩N−⟨ρK​(t),e𝐧⟩)2.\displaystyle+2\big(\langle\rho_{K}(t),e_{\bf n}\rangle_{N}-\langle\rho_{K}(t),e_{\bf n}\rangle\big)^{2}.



For the first term, by Lemma 2.2 and noting
‖e𝐧‖∞≤(2)d\|e_{\bf n}\|_{\infty}\leq(\sqrt{2})^{d}
for all 𝐧{\bf n}, we see

≤C1​N−κ​∑𝐧∈ℤd(λ𝐧+1)−α≤C2​N−κ,\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{1}N^{-\kappa}\sum_{{\bf n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}(\lambda_{\bf n}+1)^{-\alpha}\leq C_{2}N^{-\kappa},



if α>d/2\alpha>d/2 with C2=C2,T,α>0C_{2}=C_{2,T,\alpha}>0, κ>0\kappa>0. Note that the sum is bounded by
C3​(∫ℝd(π2​|x|2+1)−α​𝑑x+1)≤C4​(∫1∞r−2​α​rd−1​𝑑r+1)<∞C_{3}\big(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(\pi^{2}|x|^{2}+1)^{-\alpha}dx+1\big)\leq C_{4}\big(\int_{1}^{\infty}r^{-2\alpha}r^{d-1}dr+1\big)<\infty if α>d/2\alpha>d/2.




Next, for the second term, by Lemma 2.3, we have






∑𝐧∈ℤd(λ𝐧+1)−α​(⟨ρK​(t),e𝐧⟩N−⟨ρK​(t),e𝐧⟩)2\displaystyle\sum_{{\bf n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}(\lambda_{\bf n}+1)^{-\alpha}\big(\langle\rho_{K}(t),e_{\bf n}\rangle_{N}-\langle\rho_{K}(t),e_{\bf n}\rangle\big)^{2}

for every β∈(0,1]\beta\in(0,1].
However, since ‖ρK​(t)​e𝐧‖Cβ​(𝕋d)≤‖ρK​(t)‖Cβ​(𝕋d)​‖e𝐧‖Cβ​(𝕋d)\|\rho_{K}(t)e_{\bf n}\|_{C^{\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\leq\|\rho_{K}(t)\|_{C^{\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})}\|e_{\bf n}\|_{C^{\beta}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})} and

the above series is bounded by

Note that the last series converges if α>β+d/2\alpha>\beta+d/2.




Since ‖ρK​(t)‖Cβ≤‖ρK​(t)‖C1≤Cγ​Nγ\|\rho_{K}(t)\|_{C^{\beta}}\leq\|\rho_{K}(t)\|_{C^{1}}\leq C_{\gamma}N^{\gamma} for arbitrary small γ>0\gamma>0 by the Schauder estimates
(2.3) and K≤K¯δ​(N)K\leq\overline{K}_{\delta}(N), (2.15) is bounded by




(2.16)

C7​N−2​β+2​γ\displaystyle C_{7}N^{-2\beta+2\gamma}



for any β∈(0,1]\beta\in(0,1] such that β<α−d/2\beta<\alpha-d/2. However, by our assumption
α>d/2\alpha>d/2, we can find β>0\beta>0 small enough satisfying this condition and then we take
γ∈(0,β)\gamma\in(0,\beta). Thus, from (2.13), (2.14),
(2.15) and (2.16), we obtain the conclusion
of Theorem 1.1 by taking
κ∧(α−d/2)∧1\kappa\wedge(\alpha-d/2)\wedge 1 (with κ\kappa in Lemma 2.2)
for κ=κα\kappa=\kappa_{\alpha} in this theorem. (We can take 2​((α−d/2)∧1)−ε2((\alpha-d/2)\wedge 1)-\varepsilon
for arbitrary small ε>0\varepsilon>0 instead of (α−d/2)∧1(\alpha-d/2)\wedge 1.)
∎






2.3 Toward the proof of Theorem 2.1




The goal is now to prove Theorem 2.1, which states
hN​(t)≤C​N−κh_{N}(t)\leq CN^{-\kappa}, t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] for some C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0 and κ>0\kappa>0.
We will begin the proof of Theorem 2.1 in the next section and
will conclude in Section 8.
To clarify the main steps of the arguments, let us outline the procedure.




To give the bound on hN​(t)h_{N}(t), first in Section 3,
we calculate its time derivative ∂thN​(t)\partial_{t}h_{N}(t) in
Lemma 3.1. In this calculation, we obtain two terms
Ω1​(η)\Omega_{1}(\eta) and Ω2​(η)\Omega_{2}(\eta). The term Ω1\Omega_{1} is of diverging order O⁡(N)O(N),
while Ω2\Omega_{2} is O⁡(1)O(1) in NN but contains the diverging factor KK.
We formulate the refined one-block estimate to replace a microscopic
function Ω\Omega in general by its ensemble average in Proposition
3.2 and Corollary 3.3 for specific Ω2\Omega_{2}.




The main difficulty lies in treating the diverging term
Ω1\Omega_{1}. Sections 4 and 5 are the core of the
non-gradient and quantitative method. We prove the “gradient replacement”
in Theorem 4.1, which allows one to replace Ω1\Omega_{1}
with another O⁡(1)O(1) term, given as the term involving Pi​jP_{ij}
in Lemma 4.3.
The proof of Theorem 4.1 is reduced to the calculation of
the so-called CLT variances via a key lemma, called Kipnis-Varadhan estimate or
Itô-Tanaka trick, formulated in Lemma 4.2 in the
setting of Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics.




The CLT variances are calculated in Section 5.
In particular, Theorem 5.1, which concerns the term AℓA_{\ell},
and Proposition 5.4, which studies the minimizing sequence
{Φn}\{\Phi_{n}\} for the variational formula (1.5), play a crucial role
in showing the quantitative result for a non-gradient model.
These are taken from [14].




In Section 6, we derive an integral estimate for hN​(t)h_{N}(t)
in Lemma 6.1 as a consequence of the results obtained in Sections
3–5 and, combining it with the entropy inequality
and the large deviation type upper bound formulated in Lemma 6.2,
we obtain the estimate (6.12) for hN​(t)h_{N}(t) in Proposition
6.3. We note in Lemma 6.4 that the main
term g⁡(t)g(t) in (6.12) can be dropped if we choose the function λ⁡(t,v)\lambda(t,v)
in the local equilibrium ψt\psi_{t} according to the hydrodynamic equation (1.12).




Then, in Section 7, after summarizing all error estimates, we choose
the parameter β\beta and the mesoscopic scales nn and ℓ\ell appearing in these
estimates, and determine the second approximation F=FNF=F_{N} in ψt\psi_{t} and
find the necessary condition for K=K⁡(N)K=K(N).




The proof of Theorem 2.1 is concluded in
Section 8 by applying Gronwall’s inequality.
The proofs of several lemmas and propositions are postponed to
Section 9 and Appendices.




The procedure is outlined also in Section 1.6.






3 Time derivative of hN​(t)h_{N}(t) and one-block estimate




In this section, we first give the estimate on ∂thN​(t)\partial_{t}h_{N}(t) with
a proper error estimate to be
sufficient for our purpose, that is, to show a quantitative bound
hN​(t)≤C​N−κh_{N}(t)\leq CN^{-\kappa} with κ>0\kappa>0; see Section 3.1.
In this estimate, two terms Ω1=Ω1​(η)\Omega_{1}=\Omega_{1}(\eta) and Ω2=Ω2​(η)\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{2}(\eta)
appear. For the term Ω2\Omega_{2}, we show a “refined” one-block estimate,
that is, the local averaging property due to the local ergodicity.
“Refined” means that it provides an error estimate sufficient for our purpose
and also properly controls the diverging factor KK in Ω2\Omega_{2};
see Section 3.2.




On the other hand, the term Ω1\Omega_{1} looks of the order O⁡(N)O(N).
We need the gradient replacement which is the main part in the non-gradient method
and will be studied in Sections 4 and 5.




The results stated in this section, Lemma 3.1 and Proposition
3.2, are the extensions of known results,
providing fine error estimates, so that the proofs are postponed to Section
9. Corollary 3.3 is an application of
Proposition 3.2 to our specific Ω2​(η)\Omega_{2}(\eta).




3.1 Time derivative of the relative entropy hN​(t)h_{N}(t)




Let us begin with the calculation of the time derivative of hN​(t)h_{N}(t).
If we take K=0K=0 (i.e. no Glauber part), the following lemma is in
Lemma 3.1 of [19], but the error estimate given there was of order o⁡(1)o(1)
and it was enough to show hN​(t)=o​(1)h_{N}(t)=o(1). For our purpose, this is not
sufficient. To show hN​(t)≤C​N−κh_{N}(t)\leq CN^{-\kappa} as in Theorem 2.1,
we need to make it finer.




For ℓ∈ℕ\ell\in{\mathbb{N}} and x∈𝕋Ndx\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d} (or ℤd{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}), define

and ℓ∗:=2​ℓ+1\ell_{*}:=2\ell+1. Note that, in case of 𝕋Nd{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}, by embedding it

and therefore Λℓ,x\Lambda_{\ell,x} are well-defined
at least if ℓ∗<N\ell_{*}<N. Note that |Λℓ,x|=ℓ∗d|\Lambda_{\ell,x}|=\ell_{*}^{d}, which is
the volume of Λℓ,x\Lambda_{\ell,x}.

3.2 Refined one-block estimate




We now formulate the one-block estimate, that is, the error estimate for
the replacement
of the microscopic function Ω2\Omega_{2} of order O⁡(1)O(1) (first four terms) and O⁡(K)O(K)
(last term) by its ensemble average with mean ρ=η¯xℓ\rho=\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell} (see
(3.9) below) under the integral with respect to PftP^{f_{t}} and
also in t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T]. As we pointed repeatedly, we need to
prepare an error estimate strong enough for later use. The classical one-block
estimate can be shown even with the diverging Glauber part as discussed in
Remark 3.1 below, but it is not sufficient for our purpose.




In general for Ω∈ℱ0\Omega\in\mathcal{F}_{0}, define the error function
in the replacement of τx​Ω\tau_{x}\Omega by its ensemble mean at density
η¯xℓ\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell} as





Ω^x​(η)≡Ω^xℓ​(η)=τx​Ω​(η)−⟨Ω⟩​(η¯xℓ),\hat{\Omega}_{x}(\eta)\equiv\hat{\Omega}_{x}^{\ell}(\eta)=\tau_{x}\Omega(\eta)-\langle\Omega\rangle(\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell}),



where we denote ⟨⋅⟩​(ρ)\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle(\rho) for ⟨⋅⟩ρ=Eνρ​[⋅]\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\rho}=E^{\nu_{\rho}}[\,\cdot\,],
ρ∈[0,1]\rho\in[0,1]. Here a local sample average of η\eta over Λℓ,x\Lambda_{\ell,x}
is defined by




(3.9)

η¯xℓ≡η¯Λℓ,x:=ℓ∗−d​∑y∈Λℓ,xηy,\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell}\equiv\bar{\eta}_{\Lambda_{\ell,x}}:=\ell_{*}^{-d}\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{\ell,x}}\eta_{y},



recall (3.1) for Λℓ,x\Lambda_{\ell,x} and ℓ∗=2​ℓ+1(<N)\ell_{*}=2\ell+1\;(<N).



Proposition 3.2.



Let at,xa_{t,x}, t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] and x∈𝕋Ndx\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}, be
deterministic coefficients which satisfy




(3.10)

|at,x|≤Mfor all ​t∈[0,T],x∈𝕋Nd.|a_{t,x}|\leq M\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],\;x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}.



We assume




(3.11)

Proof.



We apply Proposition 3.2 taking each term of Ω2\Omega_{2}
as Ω\Omega.
First, note that the ensemble averages of the third and fourth terms of
Ω2\Omega_{2} vanish:





⟨cx​y​(ηx−ηy)⟩ρ=0,⟨cx​y​πx​y​Φ⟩ρ=0\langle c_{xy}(\eta_{x}-\eta_{y})\rangle_{\rho}=0,\quad\langle c_{xy}\pi_{xy}\Phi\rangle_{\rho}=0



for all Φ=Φ⁡(η)∈ℱ0\Phi=\Phi(\eta)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}.




Then, (3.16) is obtained by gathering all errors
given by (3.12) for each term of Ω2\Omega_{2}.
Indeed, take M=‖λ˙‖∞M=\|\dot{\lambda}\|_{\infty} for the first term of Ω2\Omega_{2},
M⁡(‖Ω‖∞+1)≤C​‖∂λ‖∞2​(1+‖|F|‖0,∞)2M(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1)\leq C\|\partial\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}(1+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|F\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{0,\infty})^{2}
for the second term, M⁡(‖Ω‖∞+1)≤C​‖∂2λ‖∞M(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1)\leq C\|\partial^{2}\lambda\|_{\infty}
for the third term,
M⁡(‖Ω‖∞+1)≤C​‖∂2λ‖∞​(1+‖|F|‖1,∞)M(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1)\leq C\|\partial^{2}\lambda\|_{\infty}(1+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|F\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{1,\infty})
for the fourth term and M⁡(‖Ω‖∞+1)≤C​KM(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1)\leq CK for the fifth term
noting that ρ⁡(t,v)\rho(t,v) is uniformly away from 00 and 11:
e−λ∗/(e−λ∗+1)≤ρ⁡(t,v)≤eλ∗/(eλ∗+1)e^{-\lambda_{*}}/(e^{-\lambda_{*}}+1)\leq\rho(t,v)\leq e^{\lambda_{*}}/(e^{\lambda_{*}}+1)
for λ\lambda such that ‖λ‖∞≤λ∗\|\lambda\|_{\infty}\leq\lambda_{*}.




The condition (3.17) ensures (3.13) for each choice of Ω\Omega.
In (3.16), r​(F)dr(F)^{d} needs to be (r⁡(F)+rc+1)d(r(F)+r_{c}+1)^{d}, but
this difference is absorbed to the constant CC by changing it.
∎





The proof of Proposition 3.2 is given in Section 9.2
as a combination of a known estimate adjusted to our setting, which is formulated
in Lemma 9.1, and the equivalence of ensembles with precise
convergence rate (9.17).
Proposition 3.2 is similar to Theorem 1.4 of [15] but,
differently from it, we don’t
need the relative entropy on the right-hand side of the estimate
(3.12).



Remark 3.1.



A qualitative version of Proposition 3.2 with at,x=1a_{t,x}=1:




(3.18)

limℓ→∞lim¯N→∞⁡E⁡[|∫0tN−d​∑x∈𝕋NdΩ^xℓ​(ηN​(s))​𝑑s|]=0\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\varlimsup_{N\to\infty}E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\hat{\Omega}_{x}^{\ell}(\eta^{N}(s))ds\bigg|\bigg]=0



can be shown by the classical argument. Indeed,
considering the relative entropy with respect to
the global equilibrium νN≡ν1/2N\nu^{N}\equiv\nu_{1/2}^{N} (so we take g=1g=1 in
(3.19)), we have




(3.19)

dd​t​H​(ft|1)≤−4​N2​𝒟E​(ft),\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}H(f_{t}|1)\leq-4N^{2}\mathcal{D}_{E}(\sqrt{f_{t}}),



where 𝒟E\mathcal{D}_{E} is the Dirichlet form associated with the
Kawasaki generator LEL_{E}:





𝒟E​(f)=14​∑x,y∈𝕋NdEνN​[cx,y​(η)​(f⁡(ηx,y)−f⁡(η))2](=−(f,LE​f)L2​(𝒳N,νN)).\mathcal{D}_{E}(f)=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{x,y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}E^{\nu^{N}}\Big[c_{x,y}(\eta)\big(f(\eta^{x,y})-f(\eta)\big)^{2}\Big]\;\Big(\!\!=-(f,L_{E}f)_{L^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{N},\nu_{N})}\Big).



Since a classical one-block estimate (and also two-blocks estimate) was
shown relying only on the estimate (3.19), we obtain
(3.18) even with a diverging Glauber part
in our generator ℒN\mathcal{L}_{N}; cf.  Theorem 2.7
of [13] (only for d=1d=1) or Theorem 3.1 of [19].




However, (3.18) is not enough for our purpose, instead
we prepared the stronger error estimate (3.12) in
Proposition 3.2 to show the quantitative result.




The estimate (3.19) is standard. For example, it is derived as
follows. In the estimate (4.10) of [13], taking νt=νN\nu_{t}=\nu^{N} and
L=N2​ℒNL=N^{2}\mathcal{L}_{N},
the second term on the right-hand side vanishes and also, in the first term
−𝒟⁡(ft,νN)-\mathcal{D}(\sqrt{f_{t}};\nu^{N}), one can drop the contribution of the Glauber part
since it is non-positive, and get −4​N2​𝒟E​(ft)-4N^{2}\mathcal{D}_{E}(\sqrt{f_{t}}).
This shows (3.19).







4 Gradient replacement




Sections 4 and 5 are highlights of the non-gradient
and quantitative method.




The term Ω1\Omega_{1} looks of the order O⁡(N)O(N). We need the gradient replacement
to eliminate the diverging factor in this term, with proper error estimates;
see Theorem 4.1 and Lemma 4.3.
Main estimate comes from Section 5, where we state decay estimates
for the CLT variances. In particular, Theorem 5.1 and
Proposition 5.4, taken from [14], are new and essential to
show the quantitative result. These were shown inspired by
the recent progress in the quantitative homogenization theory.




4.1 Boltzmann-Gibbs principle for gradient replacement




For the microscopic function Ω1\Omega_{1} which is given in Lemma 3.1
and looks O⁡(N)O(N), we need the following
Boltzmann-Gibbs principle for the gradient replacement.
This is a refinement of Theorem 3.2 of [19] adding the Glauber part and
giving the necessary error estimates.



Theorem 4.1.



For every T>0T>0, there exists C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0 such that





∫0t\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}
Efs[Ω1+N1−d∑x∈𝕋Nd(D(η¯xℓ)∂λ(s,x/N),ℓ∗−dτxAℓ)\displaystyle E^{f_{s}}\!\Bigg[\Omega_{1}+N^{1-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\left(D(\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell})\partial\lambda(s,x/N),\ell_{*}^{-d}\tau_{x}A_{\ell}\right)





−β​N−d2∑x∈𝕋Nd(∂λ(s,x/N),R(η¯xℓ;F)∂λ(s,x/N))]ds\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{\beta N^{-d}}{2}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\left(\partial\lambda(s,x/N),R(\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell};F)\partial\lambda(s,x/N)\right)\Bigg]\,ds





≤Cβ​K+β2​QN,ℓ(1)​(λ,F)+β​Qℓ(2)​(λ,F)\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\beta}K+\beta^{2}Q_{N,\ell}^{(1)}(\lambda,F)+\beta Q_{\ell}^{(2)}(\lambda,F)



for every t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] and β>0\beta>0, where




(4.1)

for some C>0C>0 and α1>0\alpha_{1}>0.





Combining with the key lemma stated below, the proof of Theorem
4.1 heavily relies on a recent result
(Theorem 5.1 and Corollary 5.3)
on the convergence rate of the CLT
variances. This will be discussed in Section 5.
In this section, we give the proof of Theorem 4.1
assuming that Corollary 5.3 is shown.

actually be rewritten into a term of O⁡(1)O(1).





4.2 A key lemma in the setting of Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics




We state a key lemma by which one can reduce a non-equilibrium problem
into a static problem under the canonical equilibrium measure,
sometimes called Kipnis-Varadhan estimate or Itô-Tanaka trick.
We introduce some notation. For Λ⋐ℤd\Lambda\Subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} (i.e., finite
subset of ℤd{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}) and m∈[0,|Λ|]∩ℤm\in[0,|\Lambda|]\cap{\mathbb{Z}},






𝒳Λ={0,1}Λ,𝒳Λ,m={η∈𝒳Λ;∑x∈Ληx=m},\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}=\{0,1\}^{\Lambda},\quad\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda,m}=\{\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda};\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\eta_{x}=m\},





νΛ,m= uniform probability measure on ​𝒳Λ,m,\displaystyle\nu_{\Lambda,m}=\text{ uniform probability measure on }\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda,m},



and ⟨⋅⟩Λ,m\langle\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\Lambda,m}
denotes the expectation with respect to νΛ,m\nu_{\Lambda,m}. Then,
we have a result similar to Lemmas 3.3 (the case M⁡(ρ)=0M(\rho)=0)
and 6.1 of [19], but the last term
Cβ\frac{C}{\beta} in their estimates is replaced by Cβ​K\frac{C}{\beta}K due to the
contribution from the Glauber part. We also show an estimate
for the error term instead of taking the limit in NN as in [19].




Given a positive integer ℓ\ell (recall ℓ∗=2​ℓ+1<N\ell_{*}=2\ell+1<N as stated below
(3.9)), we write Gζ=Gζ​(ξ)G_{\zeta}=G_{\zeta}(\xi) for a function
G=G⁡(η)G=G(\eta) when we consider GG as a function of ξ=η|Λ⁡(ℓ)\xi=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)}
regarding ζ=η|Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}} as a parameter. The following lemma
will be used for the proof of Theorem 4.1 taking n=dn=d
and for the proof of Lemma 4.3 (given in Section 9.4)
taking n=1n=1.



Lemma 4.2.



Let n∈ℕn\in{\mathbb{N}}, J⁡(t,v)={Ji​(t,v)}i=1n∈C∞​([0,T]×𝕋d,ℝn)J(t,v)=\{J_{i}(t,v)\}_{i=1}^{n}\in C^{\infty}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),

Then, there exists C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0
such that for every t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] and β>0\beta>0





∫0t\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}
Efs​[N1−d​∑x∈𝕋NdJ⁡(s,x/N)⋅τx​G​(η)−β​N−d​∑x∈𝕋NdJ⁡(s,x/N)⋅M⁡(η¯xℓ)​J​(s,x/N)]​d​s\displaystyle E^{f_{s}}\bigg[N^{1-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}J(s,x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}G(\eta)-\beta N^{-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}J(s,x/N)\cdot M(\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell})J(s,x/N)\bigg]\,ds





≤β​t​sup|θ|≤‖J‖∞supm,ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c[d​ℓ∗d​⟨θ⋅Gζ​(−LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ)−1​θ⋅Gζ⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m−θ⋅M⁡(m/ℓ∗d)​θ]\displaystyle\leq\beta\,t\sup_{|\theta|\leq\|J\|_{\infty}}\sup_{m,\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}}\Big[d\,\ell_{*}^{d}\big\langle\theta\cdot G_{\zeta}\,(-L_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta})^{-1}\theta\cdot G_{\zeta}\big\rangle_{\Lambda(\ell),m}-\theta\cdot M(m/\ell_{*}^{d})\theta\Big]





+Cβ​K+β2​QN,ℓ(1)​(J,G),\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{\beta}K+\beta^{2}Q_{N,\ell}^{(1)}(J,G),



where LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζL_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta} is the operator defined by




(4.5)

LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ​f​(ξ)=∑b∈(Λ⁡(ℓ))∗cb​(ξ⋅ζ)​πb​f​(ξ),\displaystyle L_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta}f(\xi)=\sum_{b\in(\Lambda(\ell))^{*}}c_{b}(\xi\cdot\zeta)\pi_{b}f(\xi),



for ξ∈𝒳Λ⁡(ℓ)\xi\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)}, ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}},

Lemma 4.2 is a combination of essentially known results,
providing fine error estimates, so the proof is postponed to

4.3 Proof of Theorem 4.1




When K=0K=0 (i.e. no Glauber part), the proof of Theorem 4.1
without error estimates was given in Sections 4–6 of [19].
Since only the Kawasaki part appears in the first term of
the estimate in Lemma 4.2
above, the arguments in Sections 4 and 5 of [19] are applicable
in our setting; see Section 5 below for more details and refined estimates.




To rewrite the function in the expectation in Theorem 4.1,
we introduce the following three ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d}-valued functions AℓA_{\ell}, BℓB_{\ell},

Wx,y​(η)=cx,y​(η)​(ηy−ηx)W_{x,y}(\eta)=c_{x,y}(\eta)(\eta_{y}-\eta_{x}) for the microscopic current,
where ei∈ℤde_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} stands for the unit vector in the ii-th direction.
Recalling ξ=η|Λ⁡(ℓ)\xi=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)} and ζ=η|Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}},

=∑i=1d∑x:τx​ei∗∈(Λ⁡(ℓ))∗τxΨi(ξ)ei,\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sum_{x:\tau_{x}e^{*}_{i}\in(\Lambda(\ell))^{*}}\tau_{x}\Psi_{i}(\xi)\;e_{i},

=−LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ​(∑x∈Λ⁡(ℓ)x​ξx),\displaystyle=-L_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta}\,\Big(\sum_{x\in\Lambda(\ell)}x\xi_{x}\Big),


and

for a given function F=(Fi)i=1d∈ℱ0dF=(F_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} which is

Recall (4.5) for the operator LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζL_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta} and

Proof of Theorem 4.1.



We apply Lemma 4.2 with
n=d,J⁡(t,v)=∂λ⁡(t,v)n=d,J(t,v)=\partial\lambda(t,v) and




(4.10)

G⁡(η)=ℓ∗−d​{D⁡(η¯ℓ)​Aℓ−Bℓ+Hℓ},M⁡(ρ)=12​{c^​(ρ,F)−c^​(ρ)}(=12​R​(ρ,F)).\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G(\eta)&=\ell_{*}^{-d}\left\{D(\bar{\eta}_{\ell})A_{\ell}-B_{\ell}+H_{\ell}\right\},\\
M(\rho)&=\frac{1}{2}\left\{\widehat{c}(\rho;F)-\widehat{c}(\rho)\right\}\;\Big(\!\!=\frac{1}{2}R(\rho;F)\Big).\end{aligned}



Note that, under the above choice, the expressions under
∫0tEfs​[⋯]​𝑑s\int_{0}^{t}E^{f_{s}}[\cdots]ds in Theorem 4.1 and
Lemma 4.2 coincide, by recalling that Ω1\Omega_{1} is defined
by (3.4) with Ωx,y\Omega_{x,y} in (3.6).




Then, we have ‖G‖∞≤C⁡(1+‖|F|‖0,∞)\|G\|_{\infty}\leq C(1+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|F\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{0,\infty}). Indeed, the function
D=D⁡(ρ)D=D(\rho) is bounded from (1.10),
‖Aℓ‖∞≤C​ℓ∗d\|A_{\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq C\ell_{*}^{d}, ‖Bℓ‖∞≤C​ℓ∗d\|B_{\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq C\ell_{*}^{d}
since Wx,yW_{x,y} is bounded, and from





Hℓ=∑{x,y}∈Λ​(ℓ)∗cx​y​(ξ⋅ζ)​πx​y​(∑z∈Λ⁡(ℓ−n)τz​F),H_{\ell}=\sum_{\{x,y\}\in\Lambda(\ell)^{*}}c_{xy}(\xi\cdot\zeta)\pi_{xy}\Big(\sum_{z\in\Lambda(\ell-n)}\tau_{z}F\Big),



with n=r⁡(F)+1n=r(F)+1, we have





‖Hℓ‖∞≤C​ℓ∗d​r​(F)d​‖F‖∞=C​ℓ∗d​‖|F|‖0,∞.\|H_{\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq C\ell_{*}^{d}r(F)^{d}\|F\|_{\infty}=C\ell_{*}^{d}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|F\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{0,\infty}.



Therefore, by (4.6), the error QN,ℓ(1)​(λ,F):=QN,ℓ(1)​(J,G)Q_{N,\ell}^{(1)}(\lambda,F):=Q_{N,\ell}^{(1)}(J,G) with JJ and GG determined as above has the estimate
(4.3).




Thus, the proof of Theorem 4.1 is concluded
with the help of Corollary 5.3, by observing that




(4.11)


Qℓ(2)​(‖J‖∞,F)\displaystyle Q_{\ell}^{(2)}(\|J\|_{\infty},F)





:=sup|θ|≤‖J‖∞supm,ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c{ℓ∗d​⟨θ⋅Gζ,(−LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ)−1​θ⋅Gζ⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m−θ⋅M⁡(m/ℓ∗d)​θ}\displaystyle\;:=\sup_{|\theta|\leq\|J\|_{\infty}}\sup_{m,\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}}\Big\{\ell_{*}^{d}\langle\theta\cdot G_{\zeta},(-L_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta})^{-1}\theta\cdot G_{\zeta}\rangle_{\Lambda(\ell),m}-\theta\cdot M(m/\ell_{*}^{d})\theta\Big\}



appearing from Lemma 4.2
is bounded by ‖J‖∞2​Qℓ​(F)\|J\|_{\infty}^{2}Q_{\ell}(F) with Qℓ​(F)Q_{\ell}(F) given in
Corollary 5.3. Taking J=∂λJ=\partial\lambda and
denoting Qℓ(2)​(λ,F):=Qℓ(2)​(‖∂λ‖∞,F)Q_{\ell}^{(2)}(\lambda,F):=Q_{\ell}^{(2)}(\|\partial\lambda\|_{\infty},F),
we obtain (4.4) in Theorem 4.1.
∎

Next, we show the following lemma, in which we evaluate the error to rewrite

Lemma 4.3.

where P⁡(ρ)≡{Pi​j​(ρ)}1≤i,j≤d:=∫0ρD⁡(ρ′)​d​ρ′P(\rho)\equiv\{P_{ij}(\rho)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq d}:=\int_{0}^{\rho}D(\rho^{\prime})d\rho^{\prime},

Then, there exists C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0 such that for every t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] and β>0\beta>0

The proof is given in Section 9.4 by carefully reviewing the
