i.e. the key lemma in the setting of Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics.






5 CLT variances and their error estimates




We now present the error estimates for the variational quantity
such as Qℓ(2)​(‖J‖∞,F)Q_{\ell}^{(2)}(\|J\|_{\infty},F) in (4.11) appearing from
Lemma 4.2. This contains the so-called CLT
(central limit theorem) variance defined by (5.1).
This quantity is defined by means
of the localized Kawasaki generator LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζL_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta} given in
(4.5) and defined on the box Λ⁡(ℓ)=Λℓ,0\Lambda(\ell)=\Lambda_{\ell,0} centered at 00,
where we denoted ζ=η|Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}} and ξ=η|Λ⁡(ℓ)\xi=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)}
for η∈𝒳\eta\in\mathcal{X}. In particular, the Glauber part plays no role and the
arguments in Sections 4 (characterization of closed forms) and 5 (computation
of variances) of [19] are valid as is in our setting.




However, these are not
sufficient for our purpose to derive appropriate decay rates for the error terms.
In particular, for the CLT variance of AℓA_{\ell}, a qualitative convergence
result as ℓ→∞\ell\to\infty (see (5.2) below)
is known and shown based on the
characterization of the closed forms, initiated by Varadhan [34]
for the Ginzburg-Landau type lattice model. But, this is weak for our purpose and,
to fill the gap,
Theorem 5.1 below was shown in [14]
by a new technique inspired by the method of the quantitative homogenization.
This theorem provides an appropriate decay rate of the CLT variance of AℓA_{\ell}
and, in particular, the characterization of the closed forms was not used
for the proof of this theorem.




The CLT variance of Bℓ−HℓB_{\ell}-H_{\ell} and the CLT covariance between AℓA_{\ell}
and Bℓ−HℓB_{\ell}-H_{\ell} are calculated in Proposition 5.2, providing
a refined error estimate. Theorem 5.1 and
Proposition 5.2 are summarized into Corollary 5.3,
which was used for the proof of Theorem 4.1.
Proposition 5.4, which is taken
from [14] and gives a decay rate for a minimizing sequence for the
variational formula (1.5), also plays a key role in our study to derive
the quantitative result in Theorem 2.1.




5.1 Definition of CLT covariances and variances




Motivated by the variational quantity appearing in Lemma 4.2,
for f,g:𝒳Λ⁡(ℓ),m→ℝf,g:\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell),m}\to{\mathbb{R}} which satisfy
⟨f⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m=⟨g⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m=0,\langle f\rangle_{\Lambda(\ell),m}=\langle g\rangle_{\Lambda(\ell),m}=0,
we define




(5.1)

Δℓ,m,ζ​(f,g)=⟨f​(−LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ)−1​g⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m,Δℓ,m,ζ​(f)=Δℓ,m,ζ​(f,f),\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}(f,g)=\langle f(-L_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta})^{-1}g\rangle_{\Lambda(\ell),m},\\
&\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}(f)=\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}(f,f),\end{aligned}



where m∈[0,ℓ∗d]∩ℤm\in[0,\ell_{*}^{d}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}} and ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}.
Recall three ℝd{\mathbb{R}}^{d}-valued functions Aℓ,BℓA_{\ell},B_{\ell} and
HℓH_{\ell}, which are defined by (4.7), (4.8), and (4.9),
respectively, and n=r⁡(F)+1n=r(F)+1 in HℓH_{\ell}.





5.2 CLT variance of AℓA_{\ell}




For the CLT variance of AℓA_{\ell}, it was shown in Theorem 5.1 of [19]
that




(5.2)

limℓ,m→∞,m/ℓ∗d→ρℓ∗−d​Δℓ,m,ζ​(θ⋅Aℓ)=2​(θ⋅c^−1​(ρ)​θ)​χ2​(ρ),\displaystyle\lim_{\ell,m\to\infty,m/\ell_{*}^{d}\to\rho}\ell_{*}^{-d}\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}(\theta\cdot A_{\ell})=2(\theta\cdot\widehat{c}^{-1}(\rho)\theta)\chi^{2}(\rho),



uniformly in ρ∈[0,1]\rho\in[0,1] and η∈𝒳\eta\in\mathcal{X},
where ζ=η|Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}.
This is the key in the gradient replacement and shown based on a
characterization of closed forms given in Corollary
4.1 of [19]. However, this is not sufficient for our purpose.
Then, recently more detailed analysis has been developed
by applying the method in the quantitative
homogenization theory and, as a by-product,
a decay rate in the limit (5.2) was obtained as in the following theorem;
see (7.12) in [14].



Theorem 5.1.



([14])
For θ∈ℝd:|θ|=1\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:|\theta|=1, m∈[0,ℓ∗d]∩ℤm\in[0,\ell_{*}^{d}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}}
and ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}, set





Qℓ(4)​(θ,m,ζ)=ℓ∗−d​Δℓ,m,ζ​(θ⋅Aℓ)−2​(θ⋅c^−1​(m/ℓ∗d)​θ)​χ2​(m/ℓ∗d).Q_{\ell}^{(4)}(\theta,m,\zeta)=\ell_{*}^{-d}\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}(\theta\cdot A_{\ell})-2(\theta\cdot\widehat{c}^{-1}(m/\ell_{*}^{d})\theta)\chi^{2}(m/\ell_{*}^{d}).



Then, there exist C>0C>0 and α1>0\alpha_{1}>0 such that




(5.3)

|Qℓ(4)​(θ,m,ζ)|≤C​ℓ−α1\displaystyle|Q_{\ell}^{(4)}(\theta,m,\zeta)|\leq C\ell^{-\alpha_{1}}



uniformly in θ∈ℝd:|θ|=1\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:|\theta|=1, m∈[0,ℓ∗d]∩ℤm\in[0,\ell_{*}^{d}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}} and
ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}.






5.3 CLT variance of Bℓ−HℓB_{\ell}-H_{\ell} and covariance of
AℓA_{\ell} and Bℓ−HℓB_{\ell}-H_{\ell}




We calculate the CLT variance of Bℓ−HℓB_{\ell}-H_{\ell} and the CLT covariance
between AℓA_{\ell} and Bℓ−HℓB_{\ell}-H_{\ell}, and provide refined error estimates
for them. These are put together in Corollary 5.3 below.



Proposition 5.2.



For F∈ℱ0dF\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} and θ,θ~∈ℝd:|θ|=|θ~|=1\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:|\theta|=|\tilde{\theta}|=1,
set





Qℓ(5)​(F)\displaystyle Q_{\ell}^{(5)}(F)
≡Qℓ(5)​(F,θ,m,ζ)\displaystyle\equiv Q_{\ell}^{(5)}(F;\theta,m,\zeta)





:=ℓ∗−d​Δℓ,m,ζ​(θ⋅(Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,F))−12​θ⋅c^​(m/ℓ∗d,F)​θ,\displaystyle:=\ell_{*}^{-d}\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}(\theta\cdot(B_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F}))-\frac{1}{2}\theta\cdot\widehat{c}(m/\ell_{*}^{d};F)\theta,




Qℓ(6)​(F)\displaystyle Q_{\ell}^{(6)}(F)
≡Qℓ(6)​(F,θ,θ~,m,ζ)\displaystyle\equiv Q_{\ell}^{(6)}(F;\theta,\tilde{\theta},m,\zeta)





:=ℓ∗−d​Δℓ,m,ζ​(θ~⋅Aℓ,θ⋅(Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,F))−(θ⋅θ~)​χ​(m/ℓ∗d).\displaystyle:=\ell_{*}^{-d}\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}(\tilde{\theta}\cdot A_{\ell},\theta\cdot(B_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F}))-(\theta\cdot\tilde{\theta})\chi(m/\ell_{*}^{d}).



Then, we have




(5.4)


|Qℓ(5)​(F)|≤C​r​(F)d​(1+‖|F|‖0,∞2)​ℓ−1,\displaystyle|Q_{\ell}^{(5)}(F)|\leq Cr(F)^{d}(1+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|F\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{0,\infty}^{2})\ell^{-1},



(5.5)


|Qℓ(6)​(F)|≤C​r​(F)2​d​‖F‖∞​ℓ−d+C​ℓ−1,\displaystyle|Q_{\ell}^{(6)}(F)|\leq Cr(F)^{2d}\|F\|_{\infty}\ell^{-d}+C\ell^{-1},



uniformly in θ,θ~∈ℝd:|θ|=|θ~|=1\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:|\theta|=|\tilde{\theta}|=1,
m∈[0,ℓ∗d]∩ℤm\in[0,\ell_{*}^{d}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}} and ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}},
where θ⋅θ~\theta\cdot\tilde{\theta} stands for the inner product of θ\theta
and θ~∈ℝd\tilde{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} which has been denoted by
(θ,θ~)(\theta,\tilde{\theta}) in the previous sections. Recall (3.2)
for ⦀F⦀0,∞{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|F\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{0,\infty}.





The proof of Proposition 5.2 is given based on known results
for Kawasaki dynamics in [19], providing careful error estimates.
The proof is simpler than that for AℓA_{\ell}, since
Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,FB_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F} is represented as LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ​GL_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta}G
for some function GG and this makes a cancellation with
(−LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ)−1(-L_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta})^{-1} in the CLT variance and the CLT covariance.
In particular, the
quantitative result from the recent homogenization theory is unnecessary
for the proof of this proposition. It is postponed to Section 9.5.





5.4 CLT variance of D​Aℓ−(Bℓ−Hℓ)DA_{\ell}-(B_{\ell}-H_{\ell})




The following corollary is shown by combining Theorem 5.1 and
Proposition 5.2, and gives a quantitative refinement of
Corollary 5.1 of [19].



Corollary 5.3.



Taking the supremum over all θ∈ℝd:|θ|=1\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:|\theta|=1,
m∈[0,ℓ∗d]∩ℤm\in[0,\ell_{*}^{d}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}} and ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}, set





Qℓ(F):=sup|θ|=1,m,ζ|ℓ∗−d\displaystyle Q_{\ell}(F):=\sup_{|\theta|=1,m,\zeta}\bigg|\ell_{*}^{-d}
Δℓ,m,ζ​(θ⋅{D⁡(m/ℓ∗d)​Aℓ−(Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,F)})\displaystyle\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}\big(\theta\cdot\{D(m/\ell_{*}^{d})A_{\ell}-(B_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F})\}\big)





−12θ⋅R(m/ℓ∗d;F)θ|,\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\theta\cdot R(m/\ell_{*}^{d};F)\theta\bigg|,



where D⁡(ρ)=c^​(ρ)/2​χ​(ρ)D(\rho)=\widehat{c}(\rho)/2\chi(\rho) is the diffusion matrix
defined in (1.8) and
R⁡(ρ,F)=c^​(ρ,F)−c^​(ρ)R(\rho;F)=\widehat{c}(\rho;F)-\widehat{c}(\rho) is the error defined in
(4.2) for c^​(ρ)\widehat{c}(\rho), which is determined by
(1.5). Then, Qℓ​(F)Q_{\ell}(F) is estimated as





Qℓ​(F)≤C⁡(1+r​(F)2​d)​(1+‖|F|‖0,∞2)​ℓ−1+C​ℓ−α1,Q_{\ell}(F)\leq C(1+r(F)^{2d})(1+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|F\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{0,\infty}^{2})\ell^{-1}+C\ell^{-\alpha_{1}},



for some C>0C>0 and α1>0\alpha_{1}>0.




Proof.



Omitting m/ℓ∗dm/\ell_{*}^{d}, we simply write D,c^,c^​(F)D,\widehat{c},\widehat{c}(F), R⁡(F)R(F) and χ\chi for D⁡(m/ℓ∗d),c^​(m/ℓ∗d),c^​(m/ℓ∗d,F)D(m/\ell_{*}^{d}),\widehat{c}(m/\ell_{*}^{d}),\widehat{c}(m/\ell_{*}^{d};F), R⁡(m/ℓ∗d,F)R(m/\ell_{*}^{d};F) and χ⁡(m/ℓ∗d)\chi(m/\ell_{*}^{d}), respectively.
Then, noting that DD is symmetric, we have





ℓ∗−d\displaystyle\ell_{*}^{-d}
Δℓ,m,ζ​(θ⋅{D​Aℓ−(Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,F)})\displaystyle\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}\big(\theta\cdot\{DA_{\ell}-(B_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F})\}\big)





=ℓ∗−d{Δℓ,m,ζ(Dθ⋅Aℓ)−2Δℓ,m,ζ(Dθ⋅Aℓ,θ⋅(Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,F))\displaystyle=\ell_{*}^{-d}\Big\{\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}\big(D\theta\cdot A_{\ell}\big)-2\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}\big(D\theta\cdot A_{\ell},\theta\cdot(B_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F})\big)





+Δℓ,m,ζ(θ⋅(Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,F))}\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}\big(\theta\cdot(B_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F})\big)\Big\}





={Qℓ(4)​(D​θ,m,ζ)+2​(D​θ⋅c^−1​D​θ)​χ2}−2​{Qℓ(6)​(F,θ,D​θ,m,ζ)+(θ⋅D​θ)​χ}\displaystyle=\big\{Q_{\ell}^{(4)}(D\theta,m,\zeta)+2\big(D\theta\cdot\widehat{c}^{-1}D\theta\big)\,\chi^{2}\big\}-2\big\{Q_{\ell}^{(6)}(F;\theta,D\theta,m,\zeta)+\big(\theta\cdot D\theta\big)\,\chi\big\}





+{Qℓ(5)​(F,θ,m,ζ)+12​(θ⋅c^​(F)​θ)}.\displaystyle\quad+\big\{Q_{\ell}^{(5)}(F;\theta,m,\zeta)+\frac{1}{2}\big(\theta\cdot\widehat{c}(F)\theta\big)\big\}.



However, by D=c^/2​χD=\widehat{c}/2\chi and c^​(F)−c^=R​(F)\widehat{c}(F)-\widehat{c}=R(F), the term except for Q(4)Q^{(4)}–Q(6)Q^{(6)} on the right-hand side
is rewritten as





2​(D​θ⋅c^−1​D​θ)​χ2−2​(θ⋅D​θ)​χ+12​(θ⋅c^​(F)​θ)=12​θ⋅R⁡(F)​θ.\displaystyle 2\big(D\theta\cdot\widehat{c}^{-1}D\theta\big)\,\chi^{2}-2\big(\theta\cdot D\theta\big)\,\chi+\frac{1}{2}\big(\theta\cdot\widehat{c}(F)\theta\big)=\frac{1}{2}\theta\cdot R(F)\theta.



Therefore, the bound for Qℓ​(F)Q_{\ell}(F) follows from (5.3),
(5.4) and (5.5) by changing the constant C>0C>0 if necessary.
∎






5.5 Minimizing sequence {Φn}\{\Phi_{n}\} for c^​(ρ)\widehat{c}(\rho) and
its estimate




The following proposition is shown from Lemma 4.4 and Proposition 6.9 of
[14] (see also Theorem 1.2 and Section 7 of [14]).
Recall (3.3) for r⁡(F)r(F), the variational formula (1.5)
for c^​(ρ)\widehat{c}(\rho) and (4.2) for R⁡(ρ,F)R(\rho;F).



Proposition 5.4.



([14])
There exists a sequence of local functions Φn=Φn​(η)∈ℱ0d\Phi_{n}=\Phi_{n}(\eta)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d} on 𝒳\mathcal{X} such that




(5.6)

r⁡(Φn)≤n,supρ∈[0,1]‖R⁡(ρ,Φn)‖≤C2​n−α2,‖Φn‖∞≤C2​n2​log⁡n,r(\Phi_{n})\leq n,\quad\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\|R(\rho;\Phi_{n})\|\leq C_{2}n^{-\alpha_{2}},\quad\|\Phi_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{2}n^{2}\log n,



for some C2>0C_{2}>0 and α2>0\alpha_{2}>0.





This proposition is a refinement of Lemma 2.1 of [19]:





infF∈ℱ0dsupρ∈[0,1]‖R⁡(ρ,F)‖=0,\inf_{F\in{\mathcal{F}}_{0}^{d}}~\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\|R(\rho;F)\|=0,



which is uniform in ρ\rho. Proposition 5.4
provides the decay rate in nn of R⁡(ρ,F)R(\rho;F) approximated by FF
of support size at most nn: r⁡(F)≤nr(F)\leq n.






6 Bound on hN​(t)h_{N}(t) combining with large deviation estimate




In this section, we first summarize the estimates obtained in
Sections 3–5 in the form of Lemma 6.1.
Then, we apply the entropy inequality and a large deviation type upper bound
under ψt\psi_{t} with fine error estimates given in Lemma 6.2.
This shows Proposition 6.3.
One can observe that the main term called g​(t)=gδ,K​(t)g(t)=g_{\delta,K}(t) in the estimate
in this proposition can be dropped if we determine the leading term of ψt\psi_{t}
according to the hydrodynamic equation (1.12) and take δ>0\delta>0 small;
see Lemma 6.4.
The equation (1.12) is used only for this lemma.




6.1 Consequence of the results of Sections 3,
4 and 5




Lemma 3.1 (calculation of ∂thN​(t)\partial_{t}h_{N}(t)),
Corollary 3.3 (refined one-block estimate)
and Theorem 4.1 (gradient
replacement, relying on the estimates for the CLT variances in Section
5) are summarized in the following lemma.
Recall hN​(t)=N−d​H​(ft|ψt)h_{N}(t)=N^{-d}H(f_{t}|\psi_{t}) in (2.2) and
ψt=ψλ⁡(t,⋅),F\psi_{t}=\psi_{\lambda(t,\cdot),F} in (2.1).
The constant C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0 may depend on TT.



Lemma 6.1.



Assume λ∈C1,3​([0,T]×𝕋d)\lambda\in C^{1,3}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}), F∈ℱ0dF\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}
and the conditions (3.8), (3.11) and (3.17).
Then, setting ρ⁡(t,v)=ρ¯​(λ⁡(t,v))\rho(t,v)=\bar{\rho}(\lambda(t,v)), we have




(6.1)

6.2 Entropy inequality and large deviation error estimate




In Lemma 6.1, Eft​[Wt]E^{f_{t}}[W_{t}] is an expectation under
Pft=ft​d​νNP^{f_{t}}=f_{t}\,d\nu^{N}.
We apply the entropy inequality to reduce it to the expectation under
Pψt=ψt​d​νNP^{\psi_{t}}=\psi_{t}\,d\nu^{N}:




(6.6)

Eft​[Wt]≤Kδ​Nd​log⁡Eψt​[eδ​Nd​K−1​Wt]+Kδ​hN​(t),E^{f_{t}}[W_{t}]\leq\frac{K}{\delta N^{d}}\log E^{\psi_{t}}[e^{\delta N^{d}K^{-1}W_{t}}]+\frac{K}{\delta}h_{N}(t),



for every δ>0\delta>0 (different from δ>0\delta>0 in K¯δ​(N)\overline{K}_{\delta}(N)).
We study the asymptotic behavior of
the first term on the right-hand side via a large deviation type upper
bound with an appropriate error estimate under
ψt​d​νN\psi_{t}\,d\nu^{N}; see Lemma 6.2 below.




For λ⁡(⋅)∈C1​(𝕋d)\lambda(\cdot)\in C^{1}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) and F=F⁡(η)∈ℱ0dF=F(\eta)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d},
dropping the tt-dependence in (2.1), the local equilibrium state
ψλ⁡(⋅),FN​(η)​d​νN\psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}(\eta)d\nu^{N} of second order approximation
is a probability measure on 𝒳N\mathcal{X}_{N} defined by




(6.7)

ψλ⁡(⋅),FN​(η)=Z−1​exp⁡{∑x∈𝕋Ndλ⁡(x/N)​ηx+1N​∑x∈𝕋Nd(∂λ⁡(x/N),τx​F​(η))},\psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}(\eta)=Z^{-1}\exp\bigg\{\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\lambda(x/N)\eta_{x}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\left(\partial\lambda(x/N),\tau_{x}F(\eta)\right)\bigg\},



for η∈𝒳N\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}, where Z=Zλ⁡(⋅),F,NZ=Z_{\lambda(\cdot),F,N} is the
normalization constant with respect to νN\nu^{N}. Then we
have the following large deviation type upper bound for
ψλ⁡(⋅),FN​d​νN\psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}\,d\nu^{N}.
A similar result is shown in Lemmas 5 and 7 of [36] in the case of F≡0F\equiv 0
and Theorem 3.3 of [19]. But here, we
give its error estimate.




The Bernoulli measure on 𝒳={0,1}ℤd\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}
associated with the chemical potential λ∈ℝ\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}} is denoted by
νλ\nu_{\lambda}, that is, νλ=ν¯λ⊗ℤd\nu_{\lambda}=\bar{\nu}_{\lambda}^{\otimes{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}, where
ν¯λ\bar{\nu}_{\lambda} is a probability measure on {0,1}\{0,1\} defined by
ν¯λ​(η):=eλ​η/(eλ+1)\bar{\nu}_{\lambda}(\eta):=e^{\lambda\eta}/(e^{\lambda}+1), η=0,1\eta=0,1.
We abuse the notation νλ\nu_{\lambda} and νρ\nu_{\rho} (defined in Section
1.1) which have different
parametrizations but are clearly distinguishable. Indeed, νλ=νρ¯​(λ)\nu_{\lambda}=\nu_{\bar{\rho}(\lambda)}, i.e. νρ\nu_{\rho} with ρ=ρ¯​(λ)\rho=\bar{\rho}(\lambda)
defined by (1.15).




The rate function for the large deviation principle for the Bernoulli measure
νλ\nu_{\lambda} is denoted by I⁡(u,λ)I(u;\lambda), namely, for u∈[0,1]u\in[0,1],




(6.8)

I⁡(u,λ)=−λ​u−q⁡(u)+p⁡(λ),p(λ)=log(eλ+1),q(u)=−{ulogu+(1−u)log(1−u)},\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &I(u;\lambda)=-\lambda u-q(u)+p(\lambda),\\
&p(\lambda)=\log(e^{\lambda}+1),\quad q(u)=-\{u\log u+(1-u)\log(1-u)\},\end{aligned}



see Appendix B for more details.




When we apply the following lemma later, we take K=K⁡(N)K=K(N), but in this lemma,
we consider KK as a parameter in the function G⁡(v,ρ)G(v,\rho),
such that 1K∈(0,1]\frac{1}{K}\in(0,1], and derive
an estimate which is uniform in 1K\frac{1}{K}.

Remark 6.1.



Compared to Theorem 2.1 of [19],
the term K​σGK\sigma_{G}, which results from the Glauber part, is new.
Since K​σGK\sigma_{G} and therefore σ\sigma contains a diverging factor KK,
we change δ⋅σ\delta\cdot\sigma to δK⋅σ\frac{\delta}{K}\cdot\sigma in the definition of
gg. From this (and when applying the entropy inequality), we have
Kδ\frac{K}{\delta} in front of ∫0tg​𝑑s\int_{0}^{t}gds
in the estimate in Proposition 6.3.
The reason for having Kδ\frac{K}{\delta} in front of ∫0thN​𝑑s\int_{0}^{t}h_{N}ds
in the estimate in Proposition 6.3 is that, when we apply
the entropy inequality, we need to pay for KK in K​σGK\sigma_{G} by the entropy factor;
recall (6.6).






6.4 g⁡(t)≤0g(t)\leq 0 for small δ>0\delta>0




In Proposition 6.3, λ=λ⁡(t,v)∈C1,3​([0,T]×𝕋d)\lambda=\lambda(t,v)\in C^{1,3}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}),
which determines hN​(t)h_{N}(t) through ψt=ψλ⁡(t,⋅),F\psi_{t}=\psi_{\lambda(t,\cdot),F},
can be arbitrary. However, in the next lemma,
we choose λ⁡(t,v)\lambda(t,v) as λ⁡(t,v)=λ¯​(ρK​(t,v))\lambda(t,v)=\bar{\lambda}(\rho_{K}(t,v)) from the solution
ρK​(t,v)\rho_{K}(t,v) of the hydrodynamic equation (1.12) and λ¯​(ρ)\bar{\lambda}(\rho)
in (1.16). Under this choice of λ⁡(t,v)\lambda(t,v) and if we take δ>0\delta>0
small enough, the integral ∫0tg⁡(s)​𝑑s\int_{0}^{t}g(s)ds in (6.12) in
Proposition 6.3 becomes non-positive, so it can be dropped.
Recall the definition of g​(t)=gδ,N​(t)g(t)=g_{\delta,N}(t) given
at the beginning of Section 6.3, especially, it depends on δ>0\delta>0.
This special choice of λ⁡(t,v)\lambda(t,v) is used only for this lemma.



Lemma 6.4.



Let ρ⁡(t,v)≡ρK​(t,v)\rho(t,v)\equiv\rho_{K}(t,v) be the solution of the hydrodynamic equation
(1.12) with ρ0\rho_{0} satisfying the condition (1.13), and define
λ⁡(t,v)≡λK​(t,v)\lambda(t,v)\equiv\lambda_{K}(t,v) as λ⁡(t,v)=λ¯​(ρ⁡(t,v))\lambda(t,v)=\bar{\lambda}(\rho(t,v)) with
λ¯​(ρ)\bar{\lambda}(\rho) in (1.16). Then, we have




(6.13)

g⁡(t)≡gδ∗,K​(t)≤0,g(t)\equiv g_{\delta_{*},K}(t)\leq 0,



for all K≥1K\geq 1 by making δ∗>0\delta_{*}>0 small enough.





When K=0K=0, this lemma is shown in (2.6) in [19] .
We can extend it to the case where K≥1K\geq 1, since we put δK\frac{\delta}{K}
in front of σ\sigma in g⁡(t)g(t). The proof is postponed to Section 9.6.

In the estimate (6.12) obtained in Proposition 6.3, we have
five error terms QN,ℓ,β,KΩ1​(λ,F)Q_{N,\ell,\beta,K}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F), QN,ℓ,δL​D​(λ,F)Q_{N,\ell,\delta}^{LD}(\lambda,F),

We choose λ=λ⁡(t,v)≡λK​(t,v)\lambda=\lambda(t,v)\equiv\lambda_{K}(t,v) and δ=δ∗>0\delta=\delta_{*}>0 as in
Lemma 6.4.
We assumed the conditions (3.8), (3.11) and (3.17)
to prove this proposition. Recall that ℓ∈ℕ\ell\in{\mathbb{N}} is taken as ℓ∗=2​ℓ+1<N\ell_{*}=2\ell+1<N
as stated below (3.9).
This section cares all these quantities and conditions, and choose

7.2 Choice of β,ℓ,F\beta,\ell,F and range of KK




Related to the error term involving R⁡(ρ,F)R(\rho;F), we recall

one can construct a sequence

r⁡(Φn)≤n,supρ∈[0,1]‖R⁡(ρ,Φn)‖≤C2​n−α2,‖Φn‖∞≤C2​n2​log⁡n,r(\Phi_{n})\leq n,\quad\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\|R(\rho;\Phi_{n})\|\leq C_{2}n^{-\alpha_{2}},\quad\|\Phi_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{2}n^{2}\log n,



for some C2>0C_{2}>0 and α2>0\alpha_{2}>0. Recall (3.3) for r⁡(Φn)r(\Phi_{n}) and

First, we choose n=n⁡(N)n=n(N) and ℓ=ℓ⁡(N)\ell=\ell(N) such that
1≤n=[Na1]≪ℓ=[Na2]≪N1\leq n=[N^{a_{1}}]\ll\ell=[N^{a_{2}}]\ll N ([⋅][\,\cdot\,] denotes the integer part)
with exponents




(7.11)

8 Proof of Theorem 2.1




In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem 2.1.
It follows from Proposition 5.4,
Proposition 6.3 and Lemma 6.4. In fact, by (6.12)
in Proposition 6.3 taking δ=δ∗>0\delta=\delta_{*}>0 and λ⁡(t,v)\lambda(t,v) determined
from (1.12) as in Lemma 6.4, F=FNF=F_{N} as given at the beginning of
Section 7.2 (as noted, this is the same as in the statement of
Theorem 2.1)
and λ∗=log⁡(1−c)/c>0\lambda_{*}=\log(1-c)/c>0 with c=min⁡{ρ−∧α−,1−ρ+∨α+}(<1/2)c=\min\{\rho_{-}\wedge\alpha_{-},1-\rho_{+}\vee\alpha_{+}\}\,(<1/2) with ρ±\rho_{\pm} and α±\alpha_{\pm} as in Lemma
11.1, we have





hN​(t)≤\displaystyle h_{N}(t)\leq
hN​(0)+Kδ∗​∫0thN​(s)​𝑑s+QN,ℓ,β,KΩ1​(λ,FN)+QN,ℓ,δ∗L​D​(λ,FN)\displaystyle h_{N}(0)+\frac{K}{\delta_{*}}\int_{0}^{t}h_{N}(s)\,ds+Q_{N,\ell,\beta,K}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,\ell,\delta_{*}}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N})





+C⁡(β+1)|∂λ|supρ∈[0,1]∞2⁡‖R⁡(ρ,FN)‖+QNE​n​(λ,FN)+QN,ℓΩ2​(λ,FN).\displaystyle+C(\beta+1)\,\|\partial\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\|R(\rho;F_{N})\|+Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,\ell}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N}).



Therefore, with the help of Gronwall’s inequality, we have




(8.1)

0≤hN​(t)≤eK​t/δ∗​(CLOSE\displaystyle 0\leq h_{N}(t)\leq e^{Kt/\delta_{*}}\Big(
hN​(0)+QN,ℓ,β,KΩ1​(λ,FN)+QN,ℓ,δ∗L​D​(λ,FN)\displaystyle h_{N}(0)+Q_{N,\ell,\beta,K}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,\ell,\delta_{*}}^{LD}(\lambda,F_{N})





+C⁡(β+1)|∂λ|supρ∈[0,1]∞2⁡‖R⁡(ρ,FN)‖\displaystyle+C(\beta+1)\,\|\partial\lambda\|_{\infty}^{2}\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\|R(\rho;F_{N})\|





OPEN+QNE​n​(λ,FN)+QN,ℓΩ2​(λ,FN)).\displaystyle+Q_{N}^{En}(\lambda,F_{N})+Q_{N,\ell}^{\Omega_{2}}(\lambda,F_{N})\Big).



Choose β=β⁡(N)\beta=\beta(N) and ℓ=ℓ⁡(N)\ell=\ell(N) as given at the beginning of
Section 7.2, and assume the condition (7.12) for
K=K⁡(N)K=K(N) taking δ=𝔠/T\delta=\mathfrak{c}/T with
𝔠>0\mathfrak{c}>0 sufficiently small. Then, as we show in Section 7.2,
each of the nine terms (recall that QN,ℓ,β,KΩ1​(λ,FN)Q_{N,\ell,\beta,K}^{\Omega_{1}}(\lambda,F_{N})
consists of four terms as in (7.2)) multiplied by eK​T/δ∗e^{KT/\delta_{*}}
on the right-hand side of (8.1) is bounded by C¯​N−κ\bar{C}N^{-\kappa}
for some C¯=C¯T>0\bar{C}=\bar{C}_{T}>0 and κ>0\kappa>0. Therefore, we conclude
hN​(t)≤C​N−κh_{N}(t)\leq CN^{-\kappa}, t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] for C=9​C¯C=9\bar{C}.
This completes the proof of Theorem 2.1.





9 Proofs of lemmas and propositions in Sections 3
– 6




Here we complete the proofs of
Lemma 3.1 (calculation of ∂thN​(t)\partial_{t}h_{N}(t)),
Proposition 3.2 (refined one-block estimate),
Lemma 4.2 (a key lemma),
Lemma 4.3 (rewriting O⁡(N)O(N)-looking term to O⁡(1)O(1)),
Proposition 5.2 (CLT variance)
and Lemma 6.4 (g⁡(t)≤0g(t)\leq 0)
postponed from Sections 3, 4,
5 and 6.
These are gathered here, since they have a common feature that
they are shown based on known results with some modification
and refinement, providing careful error estimates.




9.1 Proof of Lemma 3.1 (calculation of ∂thN​(t)\partial_{t}h_{N}(t))




Recall that hN​(t)h_{N}(t) is the relative entropy per volume defined by (2.2)
with respect to the local equilibrium ψt=ψλ⁡(t,⋅),F\psi_{t}=\psi_{\lambda(t,\cdot),F}
in (2.1). We apply the estimate




(9.1)

∂thN​(t)≤N−d​∫ψt−1​(ℒN∗​ψt−∂tψt)⋅ft​d​νN\partial_{t}h_{N}(t)\leq N^{-d}\int\psi_{t}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{L}_{N}^{*}\psi_{t}-\partial_{t}\psi_{t}\right)\cdot f_{t}\,d\nu^{N}



which holds for a large class of Markovian models, where ℒN∗\mathcal{L}_{N}^{*}
denotes the dual operator of the generator ℒN\mathcal{L}_{N} with respect to
the measure νN\nu^{N}; see [36] Lemma 1, [29] Lemma 3.1.
The proof is divided into four steps.




Step 1. (Kawasaki part)

the Kawasaki part was already computed in the proof of Lemma 3.1 of [19],
but only with the error estimate o⁡(1)o(1). Let us record the computation (3.2) in
[19] here to make

by (9.13).




Noting that EP{x}ω,0​[ηx]=ρ⁡(t,x/N)E^{P_{\{x\}}^{\omega,0}}[\eta_{x}]=\rho(t,x/N) taking F≡0F\equiv 0,

A simple estimate shows

for A0=Aω,0A_{0}=A_{\omega,0}, Z~0=Z~ω,0\widetilde{Z}_{0}=\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,0} and

by (9.11)

≤C2​N−1​r​(F)d​‖∂λ‖∞​‖F‖∞,\displaystyle\leq C_{2}N^{-1}r(F)^{d}\|\partial\lambda\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty},



if FF satisfies the condition (3.8): N−1​r​(F)d​‖∂λ‖∞​‖F‖∞≤1N^{-1}r(F)^{d}\|\partial\lambda\|_{\infty}\|F\|_{\infty}\leq 1; note that 0≤A0/Z~ω,F=A0/Z~0⋅Z~0/Z~ω,F≤ec0\leq A_{0}/\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}=A_{0}/\widetilde{Z}_{0}\cdot\widetilde{Z}_{0}/\widetilde{Z}_{\omega,F}\leq e^{c}

By (9.3), (9.6), (9.10) and (9.14),

9.2 Proof of Proposition 3.2 (refined one-block estimate)




We follow the argument in Section 3 of [15], where the same Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics ηN​(t)\eta^{N}(t) was considered. Note that although the gradient condition was assumed in [15], it was not used in Section 3.
It was used only for rewriting the term I1I_{1} in p.18 of [15]
into a form of O⁡(1)O(1) and I1I_{1} corresponds to our Ω1\Omega_{1}. (For this
correspondence, note (2.5) for 1χ⁡(ux)\frac{1}{\chi(u_{x})} appearing in I1I_{1}.)




We first decompose as




(9.15)

E⁡[|∫0tN−d​∑x∈𝕋Ndas,x​Ω^x​𝑑s|]≤\displaystyle E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}\hat{\Omega}_{x}ds\bigg|\bigg]\leq
E⁡[|∫0tN−d​∑x∈𝕋Ndas,x​mx​𝑑s|]\displaystyle E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}m_{x}ds\bigg|\bigg]





+E⁡[|∫0tN−d​∑x∈𝕋Ndas,x​Eν1/2​[Ω^x∣η¯xℓ]​𝑑s|],\displaystyle+E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}E^{\nu_{1/2}}[\hat{\Omega}_{x}\mid\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell}]ds\bigg|\bigg],



where ν1/2\nu_{1/2} is the Bernoulli measure with
ρ=1/2\rho=1/2 on 𝒳={0,1}ℤd\mathcal{X}=\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}} (recall Section 1.1)
and





mx≡mx​(η):=Ω^x−Eν1/2​[Ω^x∣η¯xℓ].\displaystyle m_{x}\equiv m_{x}(\eta):=\hat{\Omega}_{x}-E^{\nu_{1/2}}[\hat{\Omega}_{x}\mid\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell}].



Precisely saying, the three expectations in (9.15) are taken for the
process ηN​(⋅)\eta^{N}(\cdot), so that Ω^x\hat{\Omega}_{x} in the left-hand side
and mxm_{x} in the right-hand side are understood as Ω^x​(ηN​(s))\hat{\Omega}_{x}(\eta^{N}(s)) and
mx​(ηN​(s))m_{x}(\eta^{N}(s)), respectively, and also we replace η\eta to
ηN​(s)\eta^{N}(s) in the conditional expectation, which is a function of
η\eta, in the last expectation.




Lemma 3.1 of [15] applies for the first term in (9.15).
Recall we assume (3.10) for at,xa_{t,x}.



Lemma 9.1.



(Lemma 3.1 of [15]) 
Let γ=γ⁡(N)>0\gamma=\gamma(N)>0. If γ​M​ℓ∗d+2≤δ​N2\gamma M\ell_{*}^{d+2}\leq\delta N^{2}
for some δ>0\delta>0 small enough, then for every t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] and NN large enough




(9.16)

E⁡[|∫0tN−d​∑x∈𝕋Ndas,x​mx​𝑑s|]≤C⁡(Kγ+γ​ℓ∗d+2N2​M2​‖Ω‖∞2),E\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{t}N^{-d}\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}a_{s,x}m_{x}ds\bigg|\bigg]\leq C\Big(\frac{K}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma\ell_{*}^{d+2}}{N^{2}}M^{2}\|\Omega\|_{\infty}^{2}\Big),



for some C=CT>0C=C_{T}>0.




In particular, choosing
γ=NK1/2ℓ∗−(d+2)/2/M(∥Ω∥∞+1)\gamma=NK^{1/2}\ell_{*}^{-(d+2)/2}/M(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1), the right-hand side is
bounded by





2​C​N−1​K1/2​ℓ∗(d+2)/2​M​(‖Ω‖∞+1).2CN^{-1}K^{1/2}\ell_{*}^{(d+2)/2}M(\|\Omega\|_{\infty}+1).



For this choice of γ\gamma, the above condition for γ\gamma is satisfied if
(3.11) stated in Proposition 3.2 holds.




Proof.



Lemma 3.1 of [15] holds in our setting, but we need a small adjustment
in the proof. We point out the necessary changes.




Instead of the assumption (1.19) of [15]
for at,xa_{t,x} (i.e. |at,x|≤C​Kθ/[t⁡(T−t)]Θ|a_{t,x}|\leq CK^{\theta}/[t(T-t)]^{\Theta} for some θ≥0,Θ∈[0,1)\theta\geq 0,\Theta\in[0,1)), we have (3.10):





|at,x|≤Mfor all ​t∈[0,T],x∈𝕋Nd.|a_{t,x}|\leq M\quad\text{for all }t\in[0,T],\;x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}.



In other words, we may take “Θ=0\Theta=0 and MM for C​KθCK^{\theta} in
(1.19) of [15]. Furthermore, letting ε=ε⁡(N)↓0\varepsilon=\varepsilon(N)\downarrow 0,
the first term in the concluding estimate in Lemma 3.1 of [15]
becomes negligible, since ε1−Θ​Kθ=ε​M/C→0\varepsilon^{1-\Theta}K^{\theta}=\varepsilon M/C\to 0.




For the third term in the concluding estimate in Lemma 3.1
(ε2​Θ=ε0=1\varepsilon^{2\Theta}=\varepsilon^{0}=1), at the last part of its proof, we had





⟨(−Lℓ,x,j)−1​(at,x​mx),at,x​mx⟩νℓ,x,j≤1gap⁡(j,ℓ)​supt,x,η|at,x​mx|2,\langle(-L_{\ell,x,j})^{-1}(a_{t,x}m_{x}),a_{t,x}m_{x}\rangle_{\nu_{\ell,x,j}}\leq\frac{1}{\operatorname{gap}(j,\ell)}\sup_{t,x,\eta}|a_{t,x}m_{x}|^{2},



where gap⁡(j,ℓ)≥C/ℓ∗2\operatorname{gap}(j,\ell)\geq C/\ell_{*}^{2} as indicated above this
estimate in [15]. Thus, in our situation, estimating as





supt,x,η|at,x​mx|2≤(2​M)2​‖Ω‖∞2,\sup_{t,x,\eta}|a_{t,x}m_{x}|^{2}\leq(2M)^{2}\|\Omega\|_{\infty}^{2},



we obtain the estimate (9.16). Summarizing again, (9.16)
is obtained from the concluding estimate in Lemma 3.1 of [15],
by neglecting the first term (by letting ε↓0\varepsilon\downarrow 0) and replacing
C​K2​θCK^{2\theta} by (2​M)2​‖Ω‖∞2(2M)^{2}\|\Omega\|_{\infty}^{2} and ε2​Θ\varepsilon^{2\Theta} by 11.




Note that the condition ‘γ​M​ℓ∗d+2≤δ​N2\gamma M\ell_{*}^{d+2}\leq\delta N^{2}
for some δ>0\delta>0 small enough’ was used for the
denominator in (3.10) of [15] (with MM for C​KθCK^{\theta} and
ε−Θ=1\varepsilon^{-\Theta}=1), derived by Rayleigh estimate,
to stay uniformly positive, say ≥1/2\geq 1/2. This determines how small
δ>0\delta>0 needs to be. This is the condition (3.2) in [15] with
ε−Θ=ε0=1\varepsilon^{-\Theta}=\varepsilon^{0}=1 and M/CM/C instead of KθK^{\theta}.




The second assertion with the special choice of γ\gamma is straightforward.
∎





For the second term in (9.15), we apply Theorem 4.1 of [5]
(taking Λ=Λ⁡(ℓ)\Lambda=\Lambda(\ell)) which provides the equivalence of ensembles
for general Gibbs measures with precise convergence rate:
For any ε∈(0,1/4)\varepsilon\in(0,1/4), there exists C=Cε>0C=C_{\varepsilon}>0 such that for all NN
sufficiently large




(9.17)

supρ∈[0,1]max⁡supη∈𝒳Nx∈𝕋Nd⁡|Eνρ​[τx​Ω∣η¯xℓ]−⟨Ω⟩​(η¯xℓ)|≤Cℓd​|Δ|​‖Ω‖∞,\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\max_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\sup_{\eta\in\mathcal{X}_{N}}\left|E^{\nu_{\rho}}[\tau_{x}\Omega\mid\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell}]-\langle\Omega\rangle(\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell})\right|\leq\frac{C}{\ell^{d}}|\Delta|\,\|\Omega\|_{\infty},



where the local function Ω\Omega has a support in Δ\Delta such that
|Δ|≤ℓ∗d⁡(1−4​ε)|\Delta|\leq\ell_{*}^{d(1-4\varepsilon)}.




Combining the two estimates given in Lemma 9.1 and
(9.17) with ρ=1/2\rho=1/2 and |Δ|≤r​(Ω)d≤ℓ∗d⁡(1−4​ε)|\Delta|\leq r(\Omega)^{d}\leq\ell_{*}^{d(1-4\varepsilon)}
and writing the exponent as a=1−4​ε∈(0,1)a=1-4\varepsilon\in(0,1),
the proof of Proposition 3.2 is concluded.





9.3 Proof of Lemma 4.2 (a key lemma)




Recall that J⁡(t,v)∈C∞​([0,T]×𝕋d,ℝn)J(t,v)\in C^{\infty}([0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}}^{n}),
G⁡(η)∈ℱ0nG(\eta)\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{n} and M⁡(ρ)∈C⁡([0,1],ℝn⊗ℝn)M(\rho)\in C([0,1],{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{n})
are given. For a box Λ⁡(ℓ)\Lambda(\ell), we decompose η\eta as
ξ=η|Λ⁡(ℓ)\xi=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)} and ζ=η|Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta=\eta|_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}.
We write GG as G​(η)=Gζ​(ξ)G(\eta)=G_{\zeta}(\xi) regarding it as a function of
ξ\xi. We assume ⟨Gζ⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m=0\langle G_{\zeta}\rangle_{\Lambda(\ell),m}=0 for every
ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}} and m:0≤m≤ℓ∗dm:0\leq m\leq\ell_{*}^{d}.
The proof is divided into
three steps. It is given by combining that of Lemma 6.1 of [19]
and the calculation developed in that of Lemma 3.1
of [15] for Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics.




Step 1. (Application of entropy inequality and Feynman-Kac formula)
Setting





WN,t​(η)=N​∑x∈𝕋NdJ⁡(t,x/N)⋅τx​G−β​∑x∈𝕋NdJ⁡(t,x/N)⋅M⁡(η¯xℓ)​J​(t,x/N),W_{N,t}(\eta)=N\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}J(t,x/N)\cdot\tau_{x}G-\beta\sum_{x\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}J(t,x/N)\cdot M(\bar{\eta}_{x}^{\ell})J(t,x/N),



by the entropy inequality
noting that H⁡(Pf0|PνN)≤C​NdH(P_{f_{0}}|P_{\nu^{N}})\leq CN^{d} (Pf0P_{f_{0}} denotes the distribution
on the path space D⁡([0,T],𝒳N)D([0,T],\mathcal{X}_{N}) of ηN​(⋅)\eta^{N}(\cdot) with the
initial distribution f0​d​νNf_{0}\,d\nu^{N}) at the process level, we have

≤1β​N−d​log⁡EνN​[e∫0tβ​WN,s​(ηN​(s))​𝑑s]+Cβ,\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\beta}N^{-d}\log{E_{\nu^{N}}\left[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\beta W_{N,s}(\eta^{N}(s))ds}\right]}+\frac{C}{\beta},



for every β>0\beta>0; recall νN=ν1/2N\nu^{N}=\nu_{1/2}^{N} and EνN​[⋅]E_{\nu^{N}}[\,\cdot\,]
means the expectation under the process ηN​(⋅)\eta^{N}(\cdot) with the initial
distribution νN\nu^{N}.
Then, by Feynman-Kac formula, similar to (3.6) of [15]
(with ε=0,ε1−Θ=0\varepsilon=0,\varepsilon^{1-\Theta}=0 in the sense that we explained in the proof of
Lemma 9.1 in Section 9.2) shown for the
Glauber-Kawasaki dynamics, the right-hand side is bounded by




(9.18)

1β​N−d​∫0tΩN,β​(s)​𝑑s+Cβ,\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}N^{-d}\int_{0}^{t}\Omega_{N,\beta}(s)ds+\frac{C}{\beta},



where




(9.19)

ΩN,β(t)=supψ:∫ψ2​d​νN=1{EνN[βWN,tψ2]−EνN[ψ(−ℒNψ)]}.\Omega_{N,\beta}(t)=\sup_{\psi:\int\psi^{2}d\nu^{N}=1}\Big\{E^{\nu^{N}}[\beta W_{N,t}\psi^{2}]-E^{\nu^{N}}[\psi(-\mathcal{L}_{N}\psi)]\Big\}.



Note that

Step 2. (Contribution of Glauber part)
For the Glauber part, it was shown in the proof of Lemma 3.1 of [15] that





EνN​[ψ⁡(−LG​ψ)]≥−C​Nd.E^{\nu^{N}}[\psi(-L_{G}\psi)]\geq-CN^{d}.



This bound gives Cβ​K\frac{C}{\beta}K in the estimate stated in Lemma 4.2.

on the right-hand side of (9.19), then ΩN,β​(t)\Omega_{N,\beta}(t) becomes
the same quantity as (6.3) of [19] defined only from the Kawasaki part.
Then, the same bound as [19] holds
for ΩN,β​(t)\Omega_{N,\beta}(t) detached the term from Glauber part

The only difference is that,

Indeed, the first term in (9.18) dropped the contribution of the

9.4 Proof of Lemma 4.3 (rewriting O⁡(N)O(N)-looking term
to O⁡(1)O(1))




We essentially follow the proof of Lemma 3.4 of [19],

Eft​[⋯]E^{f_{t}}[\cdots]

QN,ℓ(3)​(λ)Q_{N,\ell}^{(3)}(\lambda).





9.5 Proof of Proposition 5.2 (CLT variance)




Recall (4.7), (4.8), (4.9)
for AℓA_{\ell}, BℓB_{\ell}, HℓH_{\ell} and Ψi=ηei−η0\Psi_{i}=\eta_{e_{i}}-\eta_{0}
given above (4.7). Recall also F∈ℱ0dF\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d},
θ,θ~∈ℝd:|θ|=|θ~|=1\theta,\tilde{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}:|\theta|=|\tilde{\theta}|=1,
m∈[0,ℓ∗d]∩ℤm\in[0,\ell_{*}^{d}]\cap{\mathbb{Z}} and ζ∈𝒳Λ​(ℓ)c\zeta\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda(\ell)^{c}}.
The proof is divided into three steps.




Step 1. (Known formulas)
We use the following two identities shown in the proof of Proposition 5.1
of [19]:




(9.31)


ℓ∗−d​Δℓ,m,ζ​(θ⋅(Bℓ−Hℓ))\displaystyle\ell_{*}^{-d}\Delta_{\ell,m,\zeta}\big(\theta\cdot(B_{\ell}-H_{\ell})\big)





=12​ℓ∗−d​∑b=τx​ei∗∈(Λ⁡(ℓ))∗⟨cb​[θ⋅ei​(ξx+ei−ξx)−πb​(∑y∈Λ⁡(ℓ−n)τy​F⋅θ)]2⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\ell_{*}^{-d}\sum_{b=\tau_{x}e^{*}_{i}\in(\Lambda(\ell))^{*}}\;\;\bigg\langle c_{b}\Big[\theta\cdot e_{i}(\xi_{x+e_{i}}-\xi_{x})-\pi_{b}\Big(\sum_{y\in\Lambda(\ell-n)}\tau_{y}F\cdot\theta\Big)\Big]^{2}\bigg\rangle_{\!\!\Lambda(\ell),m},

As we noted below Proposition 5.2 in Section 5.3,
these are shown noting that Bℓ,ζ−Hℓ,ζ,FB_{\ell,\zeta}-H_{\ell,\zeta,F} is represented as
LΛ⁡(ℓ),ζ​GL_{\Lambda(\ell),\zeta}G for some function GG and therefore the operator

Step 2. (Proof of (5.4))
To show (5.4), we first replace the expectation on the right-hand side

Step 3. (Proof of (5.5))
To show (5.5), we first note the exchangeability under the canonical
measure νΛ⁡(ℓ),m\nu_{\Lambda(\ell),m}, that is, η\eta and ηx,y\eta^{x,y} have the same
distributions under νΛ⁡(ℓ),m\nu_{\Lambda(\ell),m} for x,y∈Λ⁡(ℓ)x,y\in\Lambda(\ell),
x≠yx\not=y. This is easily seen from the uniformity of νΛ⁡(ℓ),m\nu_{\Lambda(\ell),m} or
the invariance of ∑z∈Λ⁡(ℓ)ηz\sum_{z\in\Lambda(\ell)}\eta_{z}
under the transform η↦ηx,y\eta\mapsto\eta^{x,y}.
In the expression (9.32), the right-hand side looks like O⁡(ℓ2​d/ℓd)O(\ell^{2d}/\ell^{d}),
but due to the exchangeability under η↦ηy,y+ei\eta\mapsto\eta^{y,y+e_{i}}, we have

if x≠y,y+eix\not=y,y+e_{i} and





⟨τy​Ψi​τx​(θ⋅F)⟩Λ⁡(ℓ),m=0\langle\tau_{y}\Psi_{i}\,\tau_{x}(\theta\cdot F)\rangle_{\Lambda(\ell),m}=0



if y,y+ei∉τx​(supp​F)y,y+e_{i}\notin\tau_{x}(\text{supp}F), i.e., y−x,y−x+ei∉supp​Fy-x,y-x+e_{i}\notin\text{supp}F,
especially, if |y−x|≥r⁡(F)+1|y-x|\geq r(F)+1.

is equal to

∑y:τy​ei∗∈(Λ⁡(ℓ))∗τyΨi=∑y:τy​ei∗∈(Λ⁡(ℓ))∗(ηy+ei−ηy)=∑z+∈∂+iΛ⁡(ℓ)ηz+−∑z−∈∂−iΛ⁡(ℓ)ηz−,\sum_{y:\tau_{y}e^{*}_{i}\in(\Lambda(\ell))^{*}}\tau_{y}\Psi_{i}=\sum_{y:\tau_{y}e^{*}_{i}\in(\Lambda(\ell))^{*}}(\eta_{y+e_{i}}-\eta_{y})=\sum_{z_{+}\in\partial_{+}^{i}\Lambda(\ell)}\eta_{z_{+}}-\sum_{z_{-}\in\partial_{-}^{i}\Lambda(\ell)}\eta_{z_{-}},

In particular, these variables are independent under νρ\nu_{\rho}.

last sum vanishes.




Summing up these calculations and bounds, we get (5.5).
This completes the proof of Proposition 5.2.

10 Schauder estimates




Here we give the proof of the Schauder estimates (2.3) for the
solution ρ=ρ⁡(t,v)≡ρK​(t,v)\rho=\rho(t,v)\equiv\rho_{K}(t,v) of (1.12). We apply the results
given in [27] for linear second order parabolic partial differential
equations (PDEs). See also
[26], but an explicit dependence of the constants in
the estimates on several data of the equation is not clearly indicated
as in (10.6), (10.7) below.




Before showing the Schauder estimates, we briefly note that the equation
(1.12) has a unique classical solution ρ⁡(t,v)∈C1,3​([0,∞)×𝕋d)\rho(t,v)\in C^{1,3}([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}) under the condition ρ0∈C4​(𝕋d)\rho_{0}\in C^{4}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) for its initial value;
recall (1.13) in which ρ0∈C5​(𝕋d)\rho_{0}\in C^{5}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) is assumed.
In fact, noting that (1.12) is a quasi-linear equation
in divergence form (0.1) in Chapter V of [26], p. ​417, by Theorem 6.1 of
[26], p. ​452, we see that it has a unique classical solution
ρ⁡(t,v)∈C1,2​([0,∞)×𝕋d)\rho(t,v)\in C^{1,2}([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}) if ρ0∈C3​(𝕋d)\rho_{0}\in C^{3}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}).
Note that the consistency condition (6.3) assumed in Theorem 6.1 is
unnecessary in our setting, since 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d} has no boundary. To obtain
C1,3C^{1,3}-property, we consider the equation for the derivative
ρ(k):=∂vkρ⁡(t,v)\rho_{(k)}:=\partial_{v_{k}}\rho(t,v) for each fixed k:1≤k≤dk:1\leq k\leq d.
By a simple calculation, we see that ρ(k)\rho_{(k)} satisfies the equation (0.1) of
[26] with






ai​(v,t,p)=∑j=1dDi​j​(ρ⁡(t,v))​pj+∑j=1dDi​j′​(ρ⁡(t,v))​∂vkρ⁡(t,v)​∂vjρ⁡(t,v),\displaystyle a_{i}(v,t,p)=\sum_{j=1}^{d}D_{ij}(\rho(t,v))p_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}D_{ij}^{\prime}(\rho(t,v))\partial_{v_{k}}\rho(t,v)\partial_{v_{j}}\rho(t,v),





a(v,t)=−f′(ρ(t,v))∂vkρ(t,v),\displaystyle a(v,t)=-f^{\prime}(\rho(t,v))\partial_{v_{k}}\rho(t,v),



where we regard that ρ⁡(t,v)\rho(t,v), ∂vkρ⁡(t,v)\partial_{v_{k}}\rho(t,v) and ∂vjρ⁡(t,v)\partial_{v_{j}}\rho(t,v)
are all given. Then, applying Theorem 6.1 of [26] again, we see that
ρ(k)∈C1,2​([0,∞)×𝕋d)\rho_{(k)}\in C^{1,2}([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}) if ∂vkρ0∈C3​(𝕋d)\partial_{v_{k}}\rho_{0}\in C^{3}({\mathbb{T}}^{d})
for each kk. This shows ρ⁡(t,v)∈C1,3​([0,∞)×𝕋d)\rho(t,v)\in C^{1,3}([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}) under the condition ρ0∈C4​(𝕋d)\rho_{0}\in C^{4}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}).




Now we show the Schauder estimates.
To apply the PDE results of [27], i.e. the estimate (10.6) below
which requires the Hölder properties of several data in the PDE,
the first step is to show the Hölder continuity of the solution of the
nonlinear PDE (1.12).




For this purpose, first in (1.12) dropping the term K​f​(ρ⁡(t,v))Kf(\rho(t,v))
and replacing D⁡(ρ⁡(t,v))D(\rho(t,v)) by D⁡(t,v)={Di​j​(t,v)}1≤i,j≤dD(t,v)=\{D_{ij}(t,v)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq d}
which is symmetric matrix-valued Borel measurable function on
[0,T]×𝕋d[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d} satisfying a bound similar to (1.10):
c∗​|θ|2≤(θ,D⁡(t,v)​θ)≤c∗​|θ|2c_{*}|\theta|^{2}\leq(\theta,D(t,v)\theta)\leq c^{*}|\theta|^{2} for θ∈ℝd\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d} with c∗,c∗>0c_{*},c^{*}>0,
we consider a solution u(0)=u(0)​(t,v)u^{(0)}=u^{(0)}(t,v) of the linear parabolic PDE




(10.1)

∂tu(0)=∑i,j=1d∂vi{Di​j​(t,v)​∂vju(0)},t≥0,v∈𝕋d,\displaystyle\partial_{t}u^{(0)}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\{D_{ij}(t,v)\partial_{v_{j}}u^{(0)}\},\quad t\geq 0,v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},



with initial value u0u_{0}. Let p⁡(t,v,s,ξ)p(t,v;s,\xi), v,ξ∈𝕋dv,\xi\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},
0≤s<t≤T0\leq s<t\leq T be the associated fundamental solution.
Then, we have the following Nash Hölder estimates; cf. [33],
[30] and related estimates
can also be found in [26].



Lemma 10.1.



There exist σ∈(0,1]\sigma\in(0,1] and C>0C>0 such that




(10.2)


|u(0)​(t1,v1)−u(0)​(t2,v2)|≤C​‖u0‖∞​(|t1−t2|1/2∨|v1−v2|(t1∧t2)1/2)σ,\displaystyle|u^{(0)}(t_{1},v_{1})-u^{(0)}(t_{2},v_{2})|\leq C\|u_{0}\|_{\infty}\Big(\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}\vee|v_{1}-v_{2}|}{(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2}}\Big)^{\sigma},


for t1,t2∈(0,T],v1,v2∈𝕋dt_{1},t_{2}\in(0,T],\;v_{1},v_{2}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d} and


(10.3)


|p⁡(t1,v1,0,ξ1)−p⁡(t2,v2,0,ξ2)|≤C(t1∧t2)d/2​(|t1−t2|1/2∨|v1−v2|∨|ξ1−ξ2|(t1∧t2)1/2)σ,\displaystyle|p(t_{1},v_{1};0,\xi_{1})-p(t_{2},v_{2};0,\xi_{2})|\leq\frac{C}{(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{d/2}}\Big(\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}\vee|v_{1}-v_{2}|\vee|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|}{(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2}}\Big)^{\sigma},



for t1,t2∈(0,T],v1,v2,ξ1,ξ2∈𝕋dt_{1},t_{2}\in(0,T],\;v_{1},v_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.




Proof.



To show (10.2), we separate into two cases: (t1∨t2)≥2​(t1∧t2)(t_{1}\vee t_{2})\geq 2(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}) and (t1∨t2)<2​(t1∧t2)(t_{1}\vee t_{2})<2(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}).
In the case (t1∨t2)≥2​(t1∧t2)(t_{1}\vee t_{2})\geq 2(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}), since |t1−t2|=(t1∨t2)−(t1∧t2)≥(t1∧t2)|t_{1}-t_{2}|=(t_{1}\vee t_{2})-(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})\geq(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}), (10.2)
is obvious with C=2C=2, noting ‖u(0)​(t)‖∞≤‖u0‖∞\|u^{(0)}(t)\|_{\infty}\leq\|u_{0}\|_{\infty} by the
maximum principle; see Lemma 11.1 below with f≡0f\equiv 0.




In the other case (t1∨t2)<2​(t1∧t2)(t_{1}\vee t_{2})<2(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}), we take t0=2​(t1∧t2)t_{0}=2(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}), R=2​(t1∧t2)1/2R=\sqrt{2}(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2}, δ=2/3\delta=\sqrt{2/3} and
x0=v1x_{0}=v_{1} in Theorem 4.1 of [30] (or Theorem II.1.8 of [33],
although it is stated only for the time-homogeneous case).
Note that t0−R2=0t_{0}-R^{2}=0 and t1,t2∈(t0−(δ​R)2,t0)=(2/3​(t1∧t2),2​(t1∧t2))t_{1},t_{2}\in(t_{0}-(\delta R)^{2},t_{0})=\big(2/3(t_{1}\wedge t_{2}),2(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})\big). Then, by Theorem 4.1
of [30], (10.2) holds if |v1−v2|≤4/3​(t1∧t2)1/2|v_{1}-v_{2}|\leq\sqrt{4/3}(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2}.
If |v1−v2|≥4/3​(t1∧t2)1/2|v_{1}-v_{2}|\geq\sqrt{4/3}(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2}, (10.2) is
obvious with C=2C=2.




To show (10.3), taking δ=(t1∧t2)1/2\delta=(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2} in Corollary 4.2
of [30] (or Corollary II.1.9 of [33]), this holds if
|v1−v2|∨|ξ1−ξ2|≤(t1∧t2)1/2|v_{1}-v_{2}|\vee|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|\leq(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2}.
Conversely, if |v1−v2|∨|ξ1−ξ2|≥(t1∧t2)1/2|v_{1}-v_{2}|\vee|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|\geq(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2},
by Aronson’s Gaussian upper bound (see Theorem 1.2 of [30]), we have





|p⁡(t1,v1,0,ξ1)−p⁡(t2,v2,0,ξ2)|\displaystyle|p(t_{1},v_{1};0,\xi_{1})-p(t_{2},v_{2};0,\xi_{2})|
≤p⁡(t1,v1,0,ξ1)+p⁡(t2,v2,0,ξ2)\displaystyle\leq p(t_{1},v_{1};0,\xi_{1})+p(t_{2},v_{2};0,\xi_{2})





≤Ct1−d/2+Ct2−d/2≤2C(t1∧t2)−d/2\displaystyle\leq Ct_{1}^{-d/2}+Ct_{2}^{-d/2}\leq 2C(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{-d/2}



and this is bounded by the right-hand side of (10.3) (with CC replaced
by 2​C2C).
∎





This lemma is used to show the Hölder continuity of the solution
ρ⁡(t,v)≡ρK​(t,v),K≥1\rho(t,v)\equiv\rho_{K}(t,v),K\geq 1 of (1.12) with ρ⁡(0)=ρ0\rho(0)=\rho_{0}.
The regularity of ρ0\rho_{0} allows to remove the singularity of ρ⁡(t,v)\rho(t,v)
near t=0t=0; see also the comment above Lemma 10.3.



Lemma 10.2.



Assume the condition (1.13) for ρ0\rho_{0}.
Then, we have




(10.4)

|ρ⁡(t1,v1)−ρ⁡(t2,v2)|≤C​K​(|t1−t2|σ/2+|v1−v2|σ),t1,t2∈[0,T],v1,v2∈𝕋d,\displaystyle|\rho(t_{1},v_{1})-\rho(t_{2},v_{2})|\leq CK\big(|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{\sigma/2}+|v_{1}-v_{2}|^{\sigma}\big),\quad t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T],\;v_{1},v_{2}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},



for some σ∈(0,1]\sigma\in(0,1]. Since D⁡(ρ)={Di​j​(ρ)}1≤i,j≤d∈C∞​([0,1])D(\rho)=\{D_{ij}(\rho)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq d}\in C^{\infty}([0,1]),
we have a similar Hölder estimate for Di​j​(ρ​(t,v))D_{ij}(\rho(t,v)).




Proof.



The proof is similar to that of Corollary 2.6 of [18] (in the discrete setting).
First, let D⁡(t,v)D(t,v) be as above and let g⁡(t,v)g(t,v) be a bounded Borel measurable
function. We consider the linear PDE





∂tu=∑i,j=1d∂vi{Di​j​(t,v)​∂vju}+g⁡(t,v),t≥0,v∈𝕋d,\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\{D_{ij}(t,v)\partial_{v_{j}}u\}+g(t,v),\quad t\geq 0,v\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d},



with u⁡(0)=u0u(0)=u_{0}. Then, by Duhamel’s formula, we have





u⁡(t,v)=u(0)​(t,v)+∫0td​s​∫𝕋dg⁡(s,ξ)​p​(t,v,s,ξ)​𝑑ξ,u(t,v)=u^{(0)}(t,v)+\int_{0}^{t}ds\int_{{\mathbb{T}}^{d}}g(s,\xi)p(t,v;s,\xi)d\xi,



where u(0)​(t,v)u^{(0)}(t,v) is the solution of (10.1). Then, by Lemma 10.1
(actually only by (10.2)),
similar to the proof of Theorem 2.2 of [18], we get




(10.5)

|u⁡(t1,v1)−u⁡(t2,v2)|≤C⁡(‖u0‖∞+‖g‖∞)​(|t1−t2|1/2+|v1−v2|(t1∧t2)1/2)σ,\displaystyle|u(t_{1},v_{1})-u(t_{2},v_{2})|\leq C(\|u_{0}\|_{\infty}+\|g\|_{\infty})\Big(\frac{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{1/2}+|v_{1}-v_{2}|}{(t_{1}\wedge t_{2})^{1/2}}\Big)^{\sigma},



for t1,t2∈(0,T],v1,v2∈𝕋dt_{1},t_{2}\in(0,T],\;v_{1},v_{2}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.




To improve the regularity near t=0t=0, we apply a trick similar to the one used
in the proof of
Theorem 2.5 of [18]. We supplementarily consider the heat equation on 𝕋d{\mathbb{T}}^{d}





∂sU=Δ​U,s∈(0,1]\partial_{s}U=\Delta U,\quad s\in(0,1]



with U⁡(0)=ρ0U(0)=\rho_{0}, and set U^​(t):=U​(1−t)\hat{U}(t):=U(1-t) and h^​(t,v):=−Δ​U^​(t,v)\hat{h}(t,v):=-\Delta\hat{U}(t,v) for t∈[0,1)t\in[0,1). Then, U^​(t,v)\hat{U}(t,v) satisfies the equation

with h⁡(0)=−Δ​U​(0)=−Δ​ρ0h(0)=-\Delta U(0)=-\Delta\rho_{0}, we see by the maximum principle that
‖h⁡(t)‖∞≤‖Δ​ρ0‖∞<∞\|h(t)\|_{\infty}\leq\|\Delta\rho_{0}\|_{\infty}<\infty by (1.13).




To apply (10.5) for our equation (1.12),
define ρ^​(t,v)\hat{\rho}(t,v), {D^i​j​(t,v)}\{\hat{D}_{ij}(t,v)\} and g^​(t,v)\hat{g}(t,v), respectively, by






ρ^(t,v)={ρ⁡(t−1,v),t∈[1,T+1],U^​(t,v),t∈[0,1).\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(t,v)=\left\{\begin{aligned} \rho(t-1,v),&\quad t\in[1,T+1],\\

where ρ⁡(t,v)≡ρK​(t,v)\rho(t,v)\equiv\rho_{K}(t,v) is the solution of (1.12)

with ρ^​(0,v)=U​(1)\hat{\rho}(0,v)=U(1). Note that ‖U⁡(1)‖∞≤‖ρ0‖∞\|U(1)\|_{\infty}\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{\infty}.
So, noting that ‖g^‖∞≤K​‖f‖∞+2​‖Δ​ρ0‖∞\|\hat{g}\|_{\infty}\leq K\|f\|_{\infty}+2\|\Delta\rho_{0}\|_{\infty}

for t1,t2∈(0,T+1],v1,v2∈𝕋dt_{1},t_{2}\in(0,T+1],\;v_{1},v_{2}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d}.
The desired bound (10.4) is obtained by restricting the above estimate
to [1,T+1][1,T+1] and shifting it by 11 in time.

Now we apply Theorem 4.8 of [27], in particular the detailed estimate given
on p. ​59 for a solution uu of the linear equation (4.22) of [27] cited below.
Before explaining it, let us first recall several Hölder norms for functions
on Ω=[0,T]×𝕋d\Omega=[0,T]\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
from pp. ​46–47 of [27]. The sup-norm on Ω\Omega is denoted by
∥⋅∥∞=|⋅|0≡|⋅|0(0)\|\cdot\|_{\infty}=|\cdot|_{0}\equiv|\cdot|_{0}^{(0)} and let α∈(0,1]\alpha\in(0,1].
The (semi) norms [u]α(b),b=0,1,|u|α(1)[u]_{\alpha}^{(b)},b=0,1,|u|_{\alpha}^{(1)} and
|u|1+α∗≡|u|1+α(0)|u|_{1+\alpha}^{*}\equiv|u|_{1+\alpha}^{(0)}
are defined for functions uu on Ω\Omega which may have a singularity like t−1/2t^{-1/2}
or others near t=0t=0, respectively, by






[u]α(b)=supX≠Y(t∧s)(α+b)/2|u⁡(X)−u⁡(Y)||X−Y|α,b=0,1,\displaystyle[u]_{\alpha}^{(b)}=\sup_{X\not=Y}(t\wedge s)^{(\alpha+b)/2}\frac{|u(X)-u(Y)|}{|X-Y|^{\alpha}},\quad b=0,1,





|u|α(1)=|u|0(1)+[u]α(1),|u|0(1)=supΩt1/2​|u⁡(X)|,\displaystyle|u|_{\alpha}^{(1)}=|u|_{0}^{(1)}+[u]_{\alpha}^{(1)},\quad|u|_{0}^{(1)}=\sup_{\Omega}t^{1/2}|u(X)|,





|u|1+α(0):=‖u‖∞+|∂vu|0(1)+[u]1+α(0)+⟨u⟩1+α(0)\displaystyle|u|_{1+\alpha}^{(0)}:=\|u\|_{\infty}+|\partial_{v}u|_{0}^{(1)}+[u]_{1+\alpha}^{(0)}+\langle u\rangle_{1+\alpha}^{(0)}





≡‖u‖∞+supΩt1/2​|∂vu⁡(X)|+supX≠Y(t∧s)(1+α)/2​|∂vu⁡(X)−∂vu⁡(Y)||X−Y|α\displaystyle\hskip 32.43611pt\equiv\|u\|_{\infty}+\sup_{\Omega}t^{1/2}|\partial_{v}u(X)|+\sup_{X\not=Y}(t\wedge s)^{(1+\alpha)/2}\frac{|\partial_{v}u(X)-\partial_{v}u(Y)|}{|X-Y|^{\alpha}}





+supX≠Y,x=y(t∧s)(1+α)/2|u⁡(X)−u⁡(Y)||X−Y|1+α,\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\sup_{X\not=Y,x=y}(t\wedge s)^{(1+\alpha)/2}\frac{|u(X)-u(Y)|}{|X-Y|^{1+\alpha}},



where X=(t,v),Y=(s,v′)∈ΩX=(t,v),Y=(s,v^{\prime})\in\Omega and |X−Y|=max⁡{|x−y|,|t−s|1/2}|X-Y|=\max\{|x-y|,|t-s|^{1/2}\}.
Note that d⁡(X,Y)=min⁡{d⁡(X),d⁡(Y)}=min⁡{t1/2,s1/2}=(t∧s)1/2d(X,Y)=\min\{d(X),d(Y)\}=\min\{t^{1/2},s^{1/2}\}=(t\wedge s)^{1/2}
and ⟨u⟩α(1)=0\langle u\rangle_{\alpha}^{(1)}=0 in the norm |u|α(1)|u|_{\alpha}^{(1)} on p. ​47 of [27].
The norm |u|1+α|u|_{1+\alpha} for functions uu without singularity near t=0t=0 is defined
on p. ​46 of [27] as





|u|1+α:=‖u‖∞+‖∂vu‖∞+supX≠Y|∂vu⁡(X)−∂vu⁡(Y)||X−Y|α+supX≠Y,x=y|u⁡(X)−u⁡(Y)||X−Y|1+α.\displaystyle|u|_{1+\alpha}:=\|u\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{v}u\|_{\infty}+\sup_{X\not=Y}\frac{|\partial_{v}u(X)-\partial_{v}u(Y)|}{|X-Y|^{\alpha}}+\sup_{X\not=Y,x=y}\frac{|u(X)-u(Y)|}{|X-Y|^{1+\alpha}}.






The detailed estimate mentioned above
clarifies the dependence of the constants in the estimate on
various data; see (10.6) below. The equation (4.22) of [27] is defined
as follows on Ω\Omega and written by dropping the sums in i,ji,j as




(4.22)

−∂tu+∂vi(ai​j∂vju+bi∂viu)+ci∂viu+c0u=∂vifi+g,\displaystyle-\partial_{t}u+\partial_{v_{i}}\big(a^{ij}\partial_{v_{j}}u+b^{i}\partial_{v_{i}}u\big)+c^{i}\partial_{v_{i}}u+c^{0}u=\partial_{v_{i}}f^{i}+g,



where ai​j,bi,fia^{ij},b^{i},f^{i} are α\alpha-Hölder continuous and ci,c0,gc^{i},c^{0},g are
bounded measurable functions on Ω\Omega that satisfy




(4.20a)


λ​|ξ|2≤ai​j​ξi​ξj≤Λ​|ξ|2,[ai​j]α(0)≤A,\displaystyle\lambda|\xi|^{2}\leq a^{ij}\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\leq\Lambda|\xi|^{2},\quad[a^{ij}]_{\alpha}^{(0)}\leq A,

‖ci‖∞≤c1,‖c0‖∞≤c2,\displaystyle\|c^{i}\|_{\infty}\leq c_{1},\quad\|c^{0}\|_{\infty}\leq c_{2},

|fi|α(1)≤F,‖g‖∞≤G,\displaystyle|f^{i}|_{\alpha}^{(1)}\leq F,\quad\|g\|_{\infty}\leq G,



for some positive constants λ,Λ,A,B,c1,c2,F\lambda,\Lambda,A,B,c_{1},c_{2},F and GG.
Then, we have

|u|1+α∗(≡|u|1+α(0))≤C⁡(d,α,λ,Λ)​(M​‖u‖∞+F+G)\displaystyle|u|_{1+\alpha}^{*}\;\big(\equiv|u|_{1+\alpha}^{(0)}\big)\leq C(d,\alpha,\lambda,\Lambda)(M\|u\|_{\infty}+F+G)



with

is a contraction under the sup-norm by the maximum principle,
we see





‖h⁡(t)‖∞≤‖Lρ0​∂vkρ0‖∞+‖Lρ0​∂vifi,(1)​(ρ0)‖∞<∞,t∈[0,1],\|h(t)\|_{\infty}\leq\|L_{\rho_{0}}\partial_{v_{k}}\rho_{0}\|_{\infty}+\|L_{\rho_{0}}\partial_{v_{i}}f^{i,(1)}(\rho_{0})\|_{\infty}<\infty,\quad t\in[0,1],



since ρ0∈C4​(𝕋d)\rho_{0}\in C^{4}({\mathbb{T}}^{d}) by the condition (1.13).
So G=‖g^‖∞≤CG=\|\hat{g}\|_{\infty}\leq C.




This shows (10.14) by applying (10.12) for the extended solution
defined on [0,T+1][0,T+1].
∎





These two lemmas also imply an estimate for ∂tρ\partial_{t}\rho.
Indeed, expanding the right-hand side of (1.12) by noting Di​j​(ρ)∈C∞​([0,1])D_{ij}(\rho)\in C^{\infty}([0,1]), and then applying (10.9) and (10.14),
we obtain




(10.16)

‖∂tρ‖∞≤C​K2​(1+σ)/σ+1.\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\rho\|_{\infty}\leq CK^{2(1+\sigma)/\sigma+1}.

11 Comparison theorem




The following lemma is standard and follows directly from the maximum principle.
Recall that f⁡(0)>0f(0)>0 and f⁡(1)<0f(1)<0 for ff in the PDE (1.12) and K>0K>0.



Lemma 11.1.



For the solution ρ⁡(t,v)\rho(t,v) of (1.12), if 0<ρ−≤ρ0​(v)≤ρ+<10<\rho_{-}\leq\rho_{0}(v)\leq\rho_{+}<1,
then we have ρ−∧α−≤ρ⁡(t,v)≤ρ+∨α+\rho_{-}\wedge\alpha_{-}\leq\rho(t,v)\leq\rho_{+}\vee\alpha_{+} for
all t≥0t\geq 0, where α−=min⁡{α∈[0,1];f⁡(α)=0}>0\alpha_{-}=\min\{\alpha\in[0,1];f(\alpha)=0\}>0 and
α+=max⁡{α∈[0,1];f⁡(α)=0}<1\alpha_{+}=\max\{\alpha\in[0,1];f(\alpha)=0\}<1.




Proof.



To show the upper bound, set w⁡(t,v):=ρ+∨α++ε−ρ⁡(t,v)w(t,v):=\rho_{+}\vee\alpha_{+}+\varepsilon-\rho(t,v)
for ε>0\varepsilon>0 small enough such that ρ+∨α++ε<1\rho_{+}\vee\alpha_{+}+\varepsilon<1. Noting
w⁡(0,v)>0w(0,v)>0, assume that there exist t0>0t_{0}>0 and v0∈𝕋dv_{0}\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d} such that
w⁡(t,v)>0w(t,v)>0 for t∈[0,t0)t\in[0,t_{0}) and w⁡(t0,v0)=0w(t_{0},v_{0})=0. Then, we have




(11.1)

∂tw⁡(t0CLOSE,\displaystyle\partial_{t}w(t_{0},
OPENv0)−∑i,j=1d∂vi{Di​j​(ρ)​∂vjw}​(t0,v0)\displaystyle v_{0})-\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\big\{D_{ij}(\rho)\partial_{v_{j}}w\big\}(t_{0},v_{0})





=−K​f​(ρ⁡(t0,v0))=−K​f​(ρ+∨α++ε)>0.\displaystyle=-Kf(\rho(t_{0},v_{0}))=-Kf(\rho_{+}\vee\alpha_{+}+\varepsilon)>0.



However, we see that ∂tw⁡(t0,v0)≤0\partial_{t}w(t_{0},v_{0})\leq 0 and, since w⁡(t0,⋅)w(t_{0},\cdot)
takes the minimum value 00 at v=v0v=v_{0}, recalling (1.9) and
(1.10) for D⁡(ρ)D(\rho), similar to [10], p. ​345, p. ​390, we have





∑i,j=1d∂vi{Di​j​(ρ)​∂vjw}​(t0,v0)≥0.\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\partial_{v_{i}}\big\{D_{ij}(\rho)\partial_{v_{j}}w\big\}(t_{0},v_{0})\geq 0.



This contradicts with (11.1) and therefore we have that
ρ⁡(t,v)<ρ+∨α++ε\rho(t,v)<\rho_{+}\vee\alpha_{+}+\varepsilon for all ε>0\varepsilon>0 and t≥0t\geq 0.
Thus the upper bound is shown. The proof for the lower bound is similar.
∎






12 Non-degeneracy and boundedness of diffusion matrix




This problem was studied in [32] in more general setting reversible
under the Gibbs measures. Nevertheless, we give the proof, in particular
that of the lower bound, since it turns out to be
explicit in the setting reversible under the Bernoulli measures.
Recall the diffusion matrix D⁡(ρ)D(\rho) defined by (1.8)
from c^​(ρ)\widehat{c}(\rho) and χ⁡(ρ)\chi(\rho)
determined by (1.5) and (1.7), respectively.



Lemma 12.1.



The bound (1.10) holds for D⁡(ρ)D(\rho), that is,





c∗​|θ|2≤(θ,D⁡(ρ)​θ)≤c∗​|θ|2,θ∈ℝd,ρ∈[0,1],\displaystyle c_{*}|\theta|^{2}\leq(\theta,D(\rho)\theta)\leq c^{*}|\theta|^{2},\quad\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\;\rho\in[0,1],



holds with c∗,c∗>0c_{*},c^{*}>0 defined by





0<c∗:=minη∈𝒳,x∈ℤd:|x|=1c0,x(η)≤c∗:=maxη∈𝒳,x∈ℤd:|x|=1c0,x(η)<∞.0<c_{*}:=\min_{\eta\in\mathcal{X},x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}:|x|=1}c_{0,x}(\eta)\leq c^{*}:=\max_{\eta\in\mathcal{X},x\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}:|x|=1}c_{0,x}(\eta)<\infty.






Proof.



The upper bound (θ,D⁡(ρ)​θ)≤c∗​|θ|2(\theta,D(\rho)\theta)\leq c^{*}|\theta|^{2}
is easily obtained by taking F=0F=0 in (1.5).
Indeed, recalling D⁡(ρ)=c^​(ρ)/2​χ​(ρ)D(\rho)=\widehat{c}(\rho)/2\chi(\rho) in (1.8)
and the variational formula (1.5) for c^​(ρ)\widehat{c}(\rho), we have





(θ,D⁡(ρ)​θ)\displaystyle(\theta,D(\rho)\theta)
≤(θ,c^​(ρ,0)​θ)/2​χ​(ρ)\displaystyle\leq(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho;0)\theta)/2\chi(\rho)





=∑|x|=1⟨c0,x​(θ,x⁡(ηx−η0))2⟩ρ/4​χ​(ρ)\displaystyle=\sum_{|x|=1}\langle c_{0,x}(\theta,x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0}))^{2}\rangle_{\rho}/4\chi(\rho)





≤c∗​∑i=1dθi2​⟨(ηei−η0)2⟩ρ/2​χ​(ρ)=c∗​|θ|2.\displaystyle\leq c^{*}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\theta_{i}^{2}\langle(\eta_{e_{i}}-\eta_{0})^{2}\rangle_{\rho}/2\chi(\rho)=c^{*}|\theta|^{2}.






To show the lower bound, noting π0,x​(ηx−η0)=−2​(ηx−η0)\pi_{0,x}(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})=-2(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})
for x:|x|=1x:|x|=1, we have





⟨(ηx−η0)​π0,x​(∑yτy​θ⋅F)⟩ρ\displaystyle\Big\langle(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})\pi_{0,x}\Big(\sum_{y}\tau_{y}\,\theta\cdot F\Big)\Big\rangle_{\rho}
=−2∑y⟨(ηx−η0)τyθ⋅F⟩ρ\displaystyle=-2\sum_{y}\langle(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})\tau_{y}\,\theta\cdot F\rangle_{\rho}





=−2∑y⟨(ηx+y−ηy)θ⋅F⟩ρ=0.\displaystyle=-2\sum_{y}\langle(\eta_{x+y}-\eta_{y})\,\theta\cdot F\rangle_{\rho}=0.



Therefore,





|θ⋅x|​⟨(ηx−η0)2⟩ρ\displaystyle|\theta\cdot x|\langle(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})^{2}\rangle_{\rho}
=|⟨(ηx−η0)​(θ⋅x⁡(ηx−η0)−π0,x​(∑yτy​θ⋅F))⟩ρ|\displaystyle=\bigg|\Big\langle(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})\Big(\theta\,\cdot x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})-\pi_{0,x}\big(\sum_{y}\tau_{y}\,\theta\cdot F\big)\Big)\Big\rangle_{\rho}\bigg|





≤⟨(ηx−η0)2⟩ρ1/2​⟨c0,xc∗​(θ⋅x⁡(ηx−η0)−π0,x​(∑yτy​θ⋅F))2⟩ρ1/2.\displaystyle\leq\langle(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})^{2}\rangle_{\rho}^{1/2}\;\Big\langle\frac{c_{0,x}}{c_{*}}\Big(\theta\cdot x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})-\pi_{0,x}\big(\sum_{y}\tau_{y}\,\theta\cdot F\big)\Big)^{2}\Big\rangle_{\rho}^{1/2}.



Then, taking the square and summing in x:|x|=1x:|x|=1 in both sides, we obtain





(2​χ​(ρ))2​∑|x|=1|θ⋅x|2≤4​χ​(ρ)c∗​(θ,c^​(ρ,F)​θ),\displaystyle(2\chi(\rho))^{2}\sum_{|x|=1}|\theta\cdot x|^{2}\leq\frac{4\chi(\rho)}{c_{*}}(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho;F)\theta),



for every F∈ℱ0dF\in\mathcal{F}_{0}^{d}. This implies the lower bound
(θ,D⁡(ρ)​θ)≥c∗​|θ|2(\theta,D(\rho)\theta)\geq c_{*}|\theta|^{2}.
∎






13 Balance condition—Examples of rates {cx,y}\{c_{x,y}\} and {cx}\{c_{x}\}




Consider the jump rates cx,y​(η)c_{x,y}(\eta) of the Kawasaki part satisfying the condition




(13.1)

cx,y​(η)=cx,y​(ηˇ),c_{x,y}(\eta)=c_{x,y}(\check{\eta}),



where ηˇ\check{\eta} is the configuration defined from η\eta by
(ηˇ)x=1−ηx(\check{\eta})_{x}=1-\eta_{x} for every xx.
Then, the corresponding diffusion matrix is symmetric in ρ\rho under the
reflection at 1/21/2,
i.e. D⁡(ρ)=D⁡(1−ρ)D(\rho)=D(1-\rho)
for ρ∈[0,1]\rho\in[0,1].
In fact, under the condition (13.1), one can rewrite
(θ,c^​(ρ,F)​θ)\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho;F)\theta\right) in the variational
formula (1.5) for c^​(ρ)\widehat{c}(\rho) as follows:





(θ,c^​(ρ,F)​θ)\displaystyle\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho;F)\theta\right)
=12​∑|x|=1⟨c0,x​(η)​(θ,x⁡(ηx−η0)−π0,x​(∑y∈ℤdτy​F​(η)))2⟩ρ\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|x|=1}\left\langle c_{0,x}(\eta)\bigg(\theta,x(\eta_{x}-\eta_{0})-\pi_{0,x}\Big(\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\tau_{y}F(\eta)\Big)\bigg)^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho}





=12​∑|x|=1⟨c0,x​(ηˇ)​(θ,x⁡(ηˇx−ηˇ0)−π0,x​(∑y∈ℤdτy​Fˇ​(ηˇ)))2⟩ρ,\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{|x|=1}\left\langle c_{0,x}(\check{\eta})\bigg(\theta,x(\check{\eta}_{x}-\check{\eta}_{0})-\pi_{0,x}\Big(\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}}\tau_{y}\check{F}(\check{\eta})\Big)\bigg)^{2}\right\rangle_{\rho},



where Fˇ​(η):=−F​(ηˇ)\check{F}(\eta):=-F(\check{\eta}).
We have used ηx−η0=−(ηˇx−ηˇ0)\eta_{x}-\eta_{0}=-(\check{\eta}_{x}-\check{\eta}_{0}) and
F​(η)=−Fˇ​(ηˇ)F(\eta)=-\check{F}(\check{\eta}).
However, under the map η↦ηˇ\eta\mapsto\check{\eta}, the Bernoulli measure
νρ\nu_{\rho} is transformed to ν1−ρ\nu_{1-\rho}. Therefore, we see
(θ,c^​(ρ,F)​θ)=(θ,c^​(1−ρ,Fˇ)​θ)\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(\rho;F)\theta\right)=\left(\theta,\widehat{c}(1-\rho;\check{F})\theta\right).
This shows c^​(ρ)=c^​(1−ρ)\widehat{c}(\rho)=\widehat{c}(1-\rho) and
D⁡(ρ)=D⁡(1−ρ)D(\rho)=D(1-\rho), since χ⁡(ρ)=ρ⁡(1−ρ)\chi(\rho)=\rho(1-\rho) is symmetric.




Note that the rate satisfying (13.1) can be constructed
as cˇx,y​(η)=(cx,y​(η)+cx,y​(ηˇ))/2\check{c}_{x,y}(\eta)=(c_{x,y}(\eta)+c_{x,y}(\check{\eta}))/2
from any rate cx,y​(η)c_{x,y}(\eta) which satisfies the non-degeneracy, locality, spatial
homogeneity and the detailed balance condition; recall (1)–(3) in Section
1.1. The new rate cˇx,y​(η)\check{c}_{x,y}(\eta) satisfies these three
conditions and (13.1).




On the other hand, one can take the flip rates cx​(η)c_{x}(\eta) of the Glauber part in
such a manner that the corresponding reaction term has the form




(13.2)

f⁡(ρ)=−c⁡(ρ−ρ−)​(ρ−1/2)​(ρ−ρ+),ρ∈[0,1],\displaystyle f(\rho)=-c(\rho-\rho_{-})(\rho-1/2)(\rho-\rho_{+}),\quad\rho\in[0,1],



for some c>0c>0 and 0<ρ−<1/2<ρ+<10<\rho_{-}<1/2<\rho_{+}<1 such that
ρ++ρ−=1\rho_{+}+\rho_{-}=1; see Example 4.1-(2) of [13].
This ff is anti-symmetric in ρ\rho under the
reflection at 1/21/2, i.e.  f⁡(1−ρ)=−f⁡(ρ)f(1-\rho)=-f(\rho), ρ∈[0,1]\rho\in[0,1].




Therefore, by the change of variable ρ=1−u\rho=1-u, we have





∫ρ−ρ+f⁡(ρ)​D​(ρ)​𝑑ρ\displaystyle\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}f(\rho)D(\rho)d\rho
=∫ρ−ρ+f⁡(1−u)​D​(1−u)​𝑑u\displaystyle=\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}f(1-u)D(1-u)du





=−∫ρ−ρ+f(u)D(u)du.\displaystyle=-\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}f(u)D(u)du.



Thus the integral should vanish, and the balance condition in Section 1.4
is satisfied in this
example, i.e., {cx,y}\{c_{x,y}\} satisfying (13.1) and {cx}\{c_{x}\}
corresponding to (13.2).




One can slightly generalize ff and {cx}\{c_{x}\} in this example.
Let us assume that the pair (D⁡(ρ),f⁡(ρ))(D(\rho),f(\rho)), determined from
certain rates {cx,y}\{c_{x,y}\} and {cx}\{c_{x}\},
satisfies that ff has three zeros 0<ρ−<ρ∗<ρ+<10<\rho_{-}<\rho_{*}<\rho_{+}<1
with stable ρ±\rho_{\pm} and unstable ρ∗\rho_{*}, and the balance condition
∫ρ−ρ+f⁡(ρ)​D​(ρ)​𝑑ρ=0\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}f(\rho)D(\rho)d\rho=0 holds. The example constructed above
satisfies this condition. Then, let us keep D⁡(ρ)D(\rho) and {cx,y}\{c_{x,y}\} as they are,
and try to find the generalization of f⁡(ρ)f(\rho) in the form:





f⁡(ρ,α)=f⁡(ρ)+∑k=0Nαk​ρk,ρ∈[0,1],f(\rho;\alpha)=f(\rho)+\sum_{k=0}^{N}\alpha_{k}\rho^{k},\quad\rho\in[0,1],



where α=(αk)k=0N∈ℝN+1\alpha=(\alpha_{k})_{k=0}^{N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1} is a parameter.




The goal is to find α∈ℝN+1\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N+1}
such that the following two conditions are satisfied:




(13.3)


f⁡(ρ+,α)=f⁡(ρ−,α)=0,\displaystyle f(\rho_{+};\alpha)=f(\rho_{-};\alpha)=0,



(13.4)


∫ρ−ρ+f⁡(ρ,α)​D​(ρ)​𝑑ρ=0.\displaystyle\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}f(\rho;\alpha)D(\rho)d\rho=0.






These two conditions can be rewritten as




(13.5)


∑k=0Nαk​(ρ+)k=∑k=0Nαk​(ρ−)k=0,\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N}\alpha_{k}(\rho_{+})^{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{N}\alpha_{k}(\rho_{-})^{k}=0,



(13.6)


∑k=0Nαk​νi​j,k=0\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{N}\alpha_{k}\nu_{ij,k}=0



for all 1≤i,j≤d1\leq i,j\leq d, where





νi​j,k:=∫ρ−ρ+ρk​Di​j​(ρ)​𝑑ρ.\displaystyle\nu_{ij,k}:=\int_{\rho_{-}}^{\rho_{+}}\rho^{k}D_{ij}(\rho)d\rho.



Since νi​j;k\nu_{ij;k} is symmetric in i,ji,j, the equation
(13.6) provides (d2+1)/2(d^{2}+1)/2 conditions.




The equations (13.5)
plus (13.6) are n:=(d2+1)/2+2n:=(d^{2}+1)/2+2 linear
homogeneous equations for (N+1)(N+1) variables {αk}k=0N\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N}.
We take NN such that N≥nN\geq n.
Then, as is well-known, for an n×(N+1)n\times(N+1) matrix AA of rank rr,
the solutions of the linear homogeneous equation A​α=0A\alpha=0 makes an
(N+1)−r(N+1)-r dimensional linear space. Since r≤nr\leq n
and N≥nN\geq n imply (N+1)−r≥1(N+1)-r\geq 1, we have at least one
non-trivial solution {αk}k=0N\{\alpha_{k}\}_{k=0}^{N} and one can make
‖α‖∞=supk|αk|\|\alpha\|_{\infty}=\sup_{k}|\alpha_{k}| to be sufficiently small.




If ‖α‖∞\|\alpha\|_{\infty} is sufficiently small, f⁡(ρ,α)f(\rho;\alpha) has only three zeros including
ρ±\rho_{\pm} and one can find the Glauber flip rates {cx}\{c_{x}\} which correspond
to this f⁡(ρ,α)f(\rho;\alpha). Indeed, to construct such rates, we may consider a linear
combination of ∏i=1kηxi\prod_{i=1}^{k}\eta_{x_{i}}, 0≤k≤N−10\leq k\leq N-1 taking different
points {x1,…,xN−1,0}\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N-1},0\} in ℤd{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} as in Example 4.1-(2) of [13].





Appendix A   rr-Markov property of ψt\psi_{t}




In this appendix, we show the rr-Markov property of ψt\psi_{t} (or PψtP^{\psi_{t}})
defined by (2.1). It was used in the proof of Lemma 3.1
in Section 9.1. In the next Lemma A.1, we denote
ψt\psi_{t} by ψ\psi for simplicity by omitting tt
and 1N\frac{1}{N} in front of the sum involving FF.
For Λ⊂𝕋Nd\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d} and r∈ℕr\in{\mathbb{N}}, set ∂rΛ={y∈𝕋Nd∖Λ;dist​(y,Λ)≤r}\partial_{r}\Lambda=\{y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}\setminus\Lambda;\text{dist}(y,\Lambda)\leq r\}, Λ¯=Λ∪∂rΛ\bar{\Lambda}=\Lambda\cup\partial_{r}\Lambda, where dist​(y,Λ)=min⁡{|x−y|;x∈Λ}\text{dist}(y,\Lambda)=\min\{|x-y|;x\in\Lambda\} is
defined in the ℓ∞\ell^{\infty} and modulo NN-sense, i.e. |x|=max1≤i≤d⁡minn∈ℤ​|xi−n​N||x|=\max_{1\leq i\leq d}\min_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}|x_{i}-nN| for x=(xi)i=1d∈𝕋Ndx=(x_{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}.
(We actually take Λ={x}\Lambda=\{x\} in
(9.11) in the proof of Lemma 3.1.)
For η∈{0,1}Λ\eta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda} and ω∈{0,1}∂rΛ\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\partial_{r}\Lambda},
η⋅ω∈{0,1}Λ¯\eta\cdot\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\bar{\Lambda}} is defined by (η⋅ω)x=ηx(\eta\cdot\omega)_{x}=\eta_{x} for x∈Λx\in\Lambda and =ωx=\omega_{x} for x∈∂rΛx\in\partial_{r}\Lambda. Define






HΛ¯(η⋅ω):=∑y∈Λ¯λ(y/N)(η⋅ω)y+∑y∈𝕋Nd:supp​τy​F⊂Λ¯(∂λ(y/N),τyF(η⋅ω)),\displaystyle H_{\bar{\Lambda}}(\eta\cdot\omega):=\sum_{y\in\bar{\Lambda}}\lambda(y/N)(\eta\cdot\omega)_{y}+\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}:\,\text{supp}\,\tau_{y}F\subset\bar{\Lambda}}(\partial\lambda(y/N),\tau_{y}F(\eta\cdot\omega)),





PΛω​(η):=Zω−1​eHΛ¯​(η⋅ω),Zω:=∑η∈{0,1}ΛeHΛ¯​(η⋅ω).\displaystyle P_{\Lambda}^{\omega}(\eta):=Z_{\omega}^{-1}e^{H_{\bar{\Lambda}}(\eta\cdot\omega)},\quad Z_{\omega}:=\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda}}e^{H_{\bar{\Lambda}}(\eta\cdot\omega)}.





Lemma A.1.



Let F∈ℱ0dF\in{\mathcal{F}}_{0}^{d} and r=r⁡(F)r=r(F). Then, ψ\psi has an rr-Markov
property, that is, for Λ⊂𝕋Nd\Lambda\subset{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d} and η∈{0,1}Λ\eta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda},




(A.1)

Pψ​([η]|ℱΛc)​(ω)=PΛω¯​(η),Pψ​-​a.s.ωP^{\psi}([\eta]|{\mathcal{F}}_{\Lambda^{c}})(\omega)=P_{\Lambda}^{\bar{\omega}}(\eta),\quad P^{\psi}\rm{\text{-}a.s.}\ \omega



holds, where [η]={ζ∈{0,1}𝕋Nd;ζ|Λ=η}[\eta]=\{\zeta\in\{0,1\}^{{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}};\zeta|_{\Lambda}=\eta\} and
ω¯=ω|∂rΛ\bar{\omega}=\omega|_{\partial_{r}\Lambda}. Recall Pψ=ψ​d​νNP^{\psi}=\psi\,d\nu^{N}.




Proof.



The lemma is well-known and the proof is elementary, but we give it
for the sake of completeness. Since the right-hand side of (A.1) is
ℱΛc{\mathcal{F}}_{\Lambda^{c}}-measurable in ω\omega, it is enough to show




(A.2)

Eψ​[f⁡(η)​g​(ω)]=Eψ​[g⁡(ω)​EPΛω¯​[f]]E^{\psi}[f(\eta)g(\omega)]=E^{\psi}\big[g(\omega)E^{P_{\Lambda}^{\bar{\omega}}}[f]\big]



for every ℱΛ{\mathcal{F}}_{\Lambda}-measurable function ff and
ℱΛc{\mathcal{F}}_{\Lambda^{c}}-measurable function gg.
The left-hand side of (A.2) is given by




(A.3)

Z−1​∑η∈{0,1}Λ,ω∈{0,1}Λcf⁡(η)​g​(ω)​eHN​(η⋅ω),Z^{-1}\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda},\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda^{c}}}f(\eta)g(\omega)e^{H_{N}(\eta\cdot\omega)},



while the right-hand side is rewritten as




(A.4)

Z−1​∑η∈{0,1}Λ,ω∈{0,1}Λcg⁡(ω)​eHN​(η⋅ω)​Zω¯−1​∑ζ∈{0,1}Λf⁡(ζ)​eHΛ¯​(ζ⋅ω¯),Z^{-1}\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda},\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda^{c}}}g(\omega)e^{H_{N}(\eta\cdot\omega)}Z_{\bar{\omega}}^{-1}\sum_{\zeta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda}}f(\zeta)e^{H_{\bar{\Lambda}}(\zeta\cdot\bar{\omega})},



where ZZ is a normalization constant (including the factor νN​(η⋅ω)≡2−N\nu^{N}(\eta\cdot\omega)\equiv 2^{-N}), and





HN​(η⋅ω):=\displaystyle H_{N}(\eta\cdot\omega):=
∑y∈𝕋Ndλ⁡(y/N)​(η⋅ω)y+∑y∈𝕋Nd(∂λ⁡(y/N),τy​F​(η⋅ω))\displaystyle\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}\lambda(y/N)(\eta\cdot\omega)_{y}+\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}}(\partial\lambda(y/N),\tau_{y}F(\eta\cdot\omega))




=\displaystyle=
HΛ¯​(η⋅ω¯)+H~Λ¯c​(η⋅ω),\displaystyle H_{\bar{\Lambda}}(\eta\cdot\bar{\omega})+\widetilde{H}_{\bar{\Lambda}^{c}}(\eta\cdot\omega),



with HΛ¯​(η⋅ω¯)H_{\bar{\Lambda}}(\eta\cdot\bar{\omega}) defined above and





H~Λ¯c(η⋅ω)=∑y∈Λ¯cλ(y/N)(η⋅ω)y+∑y∈𝕋Nd:supp​τy​F∩Λ¯c≠∅(∂λ(y/N),τyF(η⋅ω)).\widetilde{H}_{\bar{\Lambda}^{c}}(\eta\cdot\omega)=\sum_{y\in\bar{\Lambda}^{c}}\lambda(y/N)(\eta\cdot\omega)_{y}+\sum_{y\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d}:\,\text{supp}\,\tau_{y}F\cap\bar{\Lambda}^{c}\not=\emptyset}(\partial\lambda(y/N),\tau_{y}F(\eta\cdot\omega)).



However, (η⋅ω)y=ωy(\eta\cdot\omega)_{y}=\omega_{y} for y∈Λ¯cy\in\bar{\Lambda}^{c} in the first sum and
τy​F​(η⋅ω)=τy​F​(ω)\tau_{y}F(\eta\cdot\omega)=\tau_{y}F(\omega) for y∈𝕋Ndy\in{\mathbb{T}}_{N}^{d} such that
supp​τy​F∩Λ¯c≠∅\text{supp}\tau_{y}F\cap\bar{\Lambda}^{c}\not=\emptyset in the second sum, since the radius of the support of FF is rr so that
supp​τy​F∩Λ=∅\text{supp}\,\tau_{y}F\cap\Lambda=\emptyset.
Thus, H~Λ¯c​(η⋅ω)≡H~Λ¯c​(ω)\widetilde{H}_{\bar{\Lambda}^{c}}(\eta\cdot\omega)\equiv\widetilde{H}_{\bar{\Lambda}^{c}}(\omega)
is a function of ω\omega only. Therefore, in (A.4),





∑η∈{0,1}ΛeHN​(η⋅ω)=Zω¯⋅eH~Λ¯c​(ω).\sum_{\eta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda}}e^{H_{N}(\eta\cdot\omega)}=Z_{\bar{\omega}}\cdot e^{\widetilde{H}_{\bar{\Lambda}^{c}}(\omega)}.



Accordingly, the right-hand side is equal to





Z−1​∑ω∈{0,1}Λcg⁡(ω)​∑ζ∈{0,1}Λf⁡(ζ)​eHΛ¯​(ζ⋅ω¯)​eH~Λ¯c​(ω),Z^{-1}\sum_{\omega\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda^{c}}}g(\omega)\sum_{\zeta\in\{0,1\}^{\Lambda}}f(\zeta)e^{H_{\bar{\Lambda}}(\zeta\cdot\bar{\omega})}e^{\widetilde{H}_{\bar{\Lambda}^{c}}(\omega)},



which coincides with (A.3), that is the left-hand side
of (A.2). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎






Appendix B   Proof of Lemma 6.2
(large deviation type upper bound)




Lemma 6.2 gives a large deviation type upper
bound under ψλ⁡(⋅),FN​d​νN\psi_{\lambda(\cdot),F}^{N}\,d\nu^{N} defined by (6.7)
and provides an appropriate error estimate.




To prove this lemma,
we first recall some known facts on thermodynamic functions; see
[20] and Appendix B of [19] with J≡0J\equiv 0, HΛ≡0H_{\Lambda}\equiv 0
and R1=0R_{1}=0. For a finite domain Λ⋐ℤd\Lambda\Subset{\mathbb{Z}}^{d} (Λ≠∅\Lambda\not=\emptyset),
a chemical potential λ∈ℝ\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}} and particle
number k:0≤k≤|Λ|k:0\leq k\leq|\Lambda|, define grand canonical and canonical partition
functions by




(B.1)


ZΛ,λ=∑ηΛ∈𝒳Λexp⁡{λ​∑x∈Ληx}=ep⁡(λ)​|Λ|,\displaystyle Z_{\Lambda,\lambda}=\sum_{\eta_{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}}\exp\bigg\{\lambda\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\eta_{x}\bigg\}=e^{p(\lambda)|\Lambda|},

k\end{pmatrix},



respectively, where 𝒳Λ={0,1}Λ\mathcal{X}_{\Lambda}=\{0,1\}^{\Lambda} and recall (6.8)
for p⁡(λ)p(\lambda). Then,




(B.3)

as |Λ|,k→∞|\Lambda|,k\to\infty; recall (6.8) for q⁡(u)q(u).
(Note, for k=0k=0 or |Λ||\Lambda|, ZΛ,0=ZΛ,|Λ|=1Z_{\Lambda,0}=Z_{\Lambda,|\Lambda|}=1
and q⁡(0)=q⁡(1)=0q(0)=q(1)=0.) Indeed, by Stirling’s formula n!∼nn​e−n​2​π​nn!\sim n^{n}e^{-n}\sqrt{2\pi n}
(an∼bna_{n}\sim b_{n} means an/bn→1a_{n}/b_{n}\to 1 as n→∞n\to\infty), we have

−{(|Λ|−k)​log⁡(|Λ|−k)−(|Λ|−k)+12​log⁡2​π​(|Λ|−k)}+o⁡(1)\displaystyle\quad-\big\{(|\Lambda|-k)\log(|\Lambda|-k)-(|\Lambda|-k)+\tfrac{1}{2}\log 2\pi(|\Lambda|-k)\big\}+o(1)

as |Λ|,k→∞|\Lambda|,k\to\infty, and this shows (B.3).




The functions p⁡(λ)p(\lambda) and −q⁡(ρ)-q(\rho) are continuous and convex, and
satisfy




(B.4)


p⁡(λ)=supρ∈[0,1]{q⁡(ρ)+λ​ρ},\displaystyle p(\lambda)=\sup_{\rho\in[0,1]}\{q(\rho)+\lambda\rho\},

Lemma A.1.



For every λ∈ℝ\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}} and G⁡(ρ)=G1+1K​G2∈C1​([0,1])G(\rho)=G_{1}+\frac{1}{K}G_{2}\in C^{1}([0,1]),

≥a∗−p⁡(λ)−C|Λ|​(log⁡|Λ|+|λ|+‖G′‖∞).\displaystyle\geq a^{*}-p(\lambda)-\frac{C}{|\Lambda|}(\log|\Lambda|+|\lambda|+\|G^{\prime}\|_{\infty}).



We therefore have the conclusion with the estimate (B.8)
by taking Λ=Λ⁡(ℓ)\Lambda=\Lambda(\ell).
